Singular SPDEs on Homogeneous Lie Groups
Avi Mayorcas, Harprit Singh

Abstract
The aim of this article is to extend the scope of the theory of regularity structures in order to deal with a large class of singular SPDEs of the form ∂ t u = 𝔏 ​ u + F ⁡ ( u , ξ ) , \partial_{t}u=\mathfrak{L}u+F(u,\xi)\ , where the differential operator 𝔏 \mathfrak{L} fails to be elliptic. This is achieved by interpreting the base space ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} as a non-trivial homogeneous Lie group 𝔾 \mathbb{G} such that the differential operator ∂ t − 𝔏 \partial_{t}-\mathfrak{L} becomes a translation invariant hypoelliptic operator on 𝔾 \mathbb{G} . Prime examples are the kinetic Fokker-Planck operator ∂ t − Δ v − v ⋅ ∇ x \partial_{t}-\Delta_{v}-v\cdot\nabla_{x} and heat-type operators associated to sub-Laplacians. As an application of the developed framework, we solve a class of parabolic Anderson type equations ∂ t u = ∑ i X i 2 ​ u + u ⁡ ( ξ − c ) \partial_{t}u=\sum_{i}X^{2}_{i}u+u(\xi-c) on the compact quotient of an arbitrary Carnot group.



Keywords:
Regularity structures, homogeneous Lie groups, hypoelliptic operators, stochastic partial differential equations.

2020 MSC:
60L30 (Primary); 60H17, 35H10, 35K70 (Secondary)

1

Introduction

The theory of regularity structures,
[
Hai14
]
, provides a framework for the study of subcritical stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) of the form

\partial_{t}u-{\mathfrak{L}}u=F(u,\xi)\ ,

(1.1)

when the operator
{\mathfrak{L}}
is uniformly elliptic. This article extends the theory of regularity structures in order to solve equations where the differential operator stems from a large class of hypoelliptic operators.
This is achieved by building on the fundamental idea of Folland
[
Fol75
]
to reinterpret the differential operator in question as a differential operator on a homogeneous Lie group and extending the theory of regularity structures to these non-commutative spaces.

The treatment of singular SPDE via the theory of regularity structures can be divided into two parts, an analytic step and an algebraic and stochastic step.

1.

The first, analytic step, is to introduce the notions of a regularity structure, models and modelled distributions; generalised Taylor expansions of both functions and distributions. Given this set-up, sufficient analytic tools are then developed to allow for the study of abstract, fixed-point equations in the spaces of modelled distributions. Crucially, a reconstruction operator maps modelled distributions to genuine distributions (or functions) on the underlying domain. For the case of constant coefficient, hypoelliptic equations on compact quotients of Euclidean domains, this aspect of the theory was already worked out in full generality in
[
Hai14
]
.

2.

The second step, which was carried out in a case by case basis in
[
Hai14
]
, was then fully automated in the subsequent works
[
CH16
,
BHZ19
,
BCCH21
]
.
In
[
BHZ19
]
the authors construct concrete (equation-dependent) regularity structures and models together with a large enough renormalisation group.
Then, in
[
BCCH21
]
, the question of how this renormalisation group acts on the SPDE in question was answered. Convergence of renormalised models for an extremely large class of noises, known as the BPHZ theorem, was proved in
[
CH16
]
.

In much of the above work translation invariance on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, of both the equation and the driving noise, plays a major role. In this paper we will instead work with equations that are translation invariant with respect to a homogeneous Lie group
\mathbb{G}
(Euclidean spaces being special case).

The bulk of this paper is dedicated to implementing the analytic aspects of the theory in the case when the underlying space is a general (non-Abelian) homogeneous Lie group. The second step is then carried out for a specific class of equations. While the theory developed herein remains, somewhat surprisingly, close to that of
[
Hai14
]
, there are a number of key conceptual and technical obstacles to overcome. The main cause of these deviations are the non-commutative structure of the base space and the fact that the notion of Taylor expansions, a crucial element of the theory, heavily depends on the underlying group structure. We discuss these issues in more detail in Section
1.3
along with some wider context in Section
1.4
. For the readers convenience the article aims at being self-contained and thus arguments that adapt directly from
[
Hai14
]
are clearly marked as such.

The central motivation for this extension to homogeneous Lie groups is to allow for the study of singular SPDE with linear part given by a hypoelliptic operator, which may fail to be uniformly parabolic. Two motivating examples are the heat operator associated to the sub-Laplacian on the Heisenberg group and the kinetic Fokker-Planck operator on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,

u(t,x,y,z)\mapsto\partial_{t}u-(\Delta_{x}+\Delta_{y}+(x^{2}+y^{2})\Delta_{z})u\text{ and }u(t,x,v)\mapsto\partial_{t}u-\Delta_{v}u-v\cdot\nabla_{x}u\ .

(1.2)

We will carry these two operators and their associated homogeneous Lie groups, throughout the paper as working examples of the theory. In the final sections we develop a full solution theory for Anderson type equations associated to sub-Laplacians on general stratified Lie groups (Carnot groups), of which the Heisenberg group is a well-studied example.

More generally, however, the analytic results of this paper apply to any differential operator,
\bar{\mathfrak{L}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, such that the following combination of results by Folland applies to
\mathfrak{L}:=\partial_{t}-\bar{\mathfrak{L}}
, with respect to some homogeneous Lie group structure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Theorem 1.1

(
[
Fol75
, Thm. 2.1 & Cor. 2.8]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a homogeneous Lie group of homogeneous dimension
|\mathfrak{s}|
and
\mathfrak{L}
be a left-translation invariant (with respect to
\mathbb{G}
), homogeneous differential operator of degree
\beta\in(0,|\mathfrak{s}|)
on
\mathbb{G}
, such that
\mathfrak{L}
and its adjoint
\mathfrak{L}^{\ast}
are both hypoelliptic. Then there exists a unique homogeneous distribution
K
, of order
\beta-|\mathfrak{s}|
such that for any distribution,
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{G})

\mathfrak{L}(\varphi\ast K)=(\mathfrak{L}\varphi)\ast K=\varphi.

where the convolution is with respect to the given Lie structure.

Referring to Section
2
for a more thorough discussion of homogeneous Lie groups and their properties, we recall here that a differential operator
\mathfrak{D}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called
hypoelliptic
, if for every open subset
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
one has that,

\mathfrak{D}u\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)\Rightarrow u\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)\ .

The following celebrated result of Hörmander (almost) entirely classifies the family of second order hypoelliptic operators.

Theorem 1.2

(
[
Hö67
, Thm. 1.1]
)
.

Let
r\leq d
and
\{X_{i}\}_{i=0}^{r}
be a collection of first order differential operators (i.e. vector fields) on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and recursively define
\mathcal{V}_{1}=\text{span}\{X_{i}\,:\,i=0,\ldots,r\}
,
\mathcal{V}_{n+1}=\mathcal{V}_{n}\cup\left\{[V,W]\,:V\in\mathcal{V}_{1},\,W\in\mathcal{V}_{n}\right\}
. If a differential operator can be written in the form,

\mathfrak{D}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}X_{i}^{2}+X_{0}+c,

(1.3)

for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
, and there exists an
N\geq 1
such that
\dim(\mathcal{V}_{N})=d
at every point in
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
then
\mathfrak{D}
is hypoelliptic on
\Omega
.

Remark 1.3
.

By Frobenius’s theorem,
[
Fro77
]
, if the condition of Theorem
1.2
fails in some open set then
\mathfrak{D}
is not hypoelliptic on that set. However, the statement is not truly sharp; for example the Grushin operator
\partial^{2}_{vv}-v\partial_{x}
is hypoelliptic, while the conditions of Theorem
1.2
are violated on sets intersecting
\{v=0\}
. On the other hand this type of exception cannot occur if the sections
\mathcal{V}_{i}
are continuous, since in this case it can be shown that the map
x\mapsto\mathbb{1}_{\{\dim\mathcal{V}_{i}(x)=d\}}
is upper semi-continuous.

While Hörmander’s theorem gives an almost sharp
characterisation of second order, hypoelliptic operators it does not say much about fine properties of the fundamental solution. For example, it gives no direct route to a refined regularity theory for hypoelliptic equations. This observation highlights the contribution of Folland’s theorem (Theorem
1.1
); since translation invariance allows access to many additional tools in the Euclidean setting, such as harmonic analysis and singular integral methods. Viewing the class of translation invariant operators satisfying Folland’s theorem as analogous to constant coefficient, elliptic operators on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
a programme was successfully carried out in the works
[
FS74a
,
FS74b
,
Fol75
,
RS76
]
, establishing a full
L^{p}
regularity theory for general, smooth coefficient, hypoelliptic operators. We refer to
[
Bra14
, Ch. 3]
for a concise introduction to this programme.

1.1

Related Literature

Non-translation invariant and non-uniformly elliptic SPDEs have been well-studied in the classical, i.e. non-singular, regime and we do not attempt to present this large literature here. We refer to standard texts such as
[
DPZ14
,
LR17
,
DKM+09
,
PR07
]
for a general overview and references to more specific works contained therein. However, in the more specialised setting of semi-linear SPDEs on homogeneous Lie groups, we mention the works
[
TV99
,
TV02
,
PT10
]
which treat both hypoelliptic and parabolic SPDEs on some classes of Lie groups and sub-Riemannian manifolds. A solution theory for conservative SPDEs based on the kinetic Fokker–Planck equation (see Section
4.4
) in the Itô case was developed in
[
FFPV17
]
. Using the theory of paracontrolled calculus this was extended to the singular regime in
[
HZZZ24
]
. The kinetic Fokker–Plank operator and its associated homogeneous Lie group fall into the analytic framework developed in this paper, however, we postpone a concrete application of this theory towards a kinetic Anderson type equation to a future work. Recently, in
[
BOTW23
]
, an Anderson type equation on the Heisenberg group with white in time and coloured in space noise was studied using Itô calculus techniques, up the to the full sub-critical regime of the noise regularity. We treat a closely related problem in the final sections of this paper. A fuller discussion of the similarities and differences between the results of
[
BOTW23
]
and those of our approach is postponed to Remark
6.7
.

Singular SPDEs with non-translation invariant, but uniformly parabolic or elliptic linear part, have also been considered, especially using the recently developed pathwise techniques of
[
Hai14
,
GIP15
,
OSSW21
,
Sin23
]
. Some of these works are discussed in more detail in Section
1.4
below. Quasilinear SPDEs have been studied using regularity structures, rough path based methods and paracontrolled distribution theory, see
[
GH19b
,
OW19
,
BM22
,
BHK23
]
. Recently, an approach inspired by the theory of regularity structures, but technically distinct, has been developed in
[
OSSW21
,
LOT21
,
LO22
]
. SPDEs on domains with boundaries have been treated using both regularity structures and paracontrolled distribution based methods,
[
Lab19
,
GH19a
,
CvZ21
,
GH21
]
. Finally, a number of works have considered parabolic, singular SPDEs on Riemannian manifolds; a paracontrolled approach using the spectral decomposition associated to the Laplace–Beltrami operator has been developed in
[
BB16
,
BB19
,
Ant22
]
. An approach via regularity structures has been applied to the
2
d parabolic Anderson equation on a Riemannian manifold in
[
DDD19
]
, while
[
BB21
,
Sin23
,
HS25
,
CX23
]
develop some aspects required to treat non-translation invariant, uniformly parabolic equations. Finally, the work
[
HS23
]
gives a comprehensive extension of regularity structures to singular SPDEs on Riemannian manifolds, with only the renormalisation of suitable stochastic objects left to a case by case treatment.

1.2

A Motivating Class of Examples

In this paper we restrict ourselves to linear operators satisfying the criteria of Theorem
1.1
and use the Anderson equation as a main motivating example, although we stress that our main analytic results apply in the full generality treated by
[
Hai14
]
. The parabolic Anderson model

\partial_{t}u=\Delta u-u\xi,\quad u|_{t=0}=u_{0},

(1.4)

on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
describes the conditional, expected density of particles, where each particle moves according to an independent Brownian motion and branches at a rate proportional to the random environment
\xi
, see
[
Kön16
,
CM94
]
. Rigorously, this description is derived by discrete approximations and it is well known that in the case
\xi
is a spatial white noise, when
d=2
and
d=3
one needs to recentre the potential in order to obtain a non-trivial limit as the discretisation is removed,
[
Hai14
,
HL15
,
HL18
,
GIP15
]
. We point out that if the environment is also allowed to depend on time and is for example, white in time, then martingale methods can be used instead to develop a probabilistic solution theory, see
[
Wal86
,
Dal99
,
Dal01
,
Che15
]
.

If one replaces the Brownian motions with a general diffusion,

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{t}=\sqrt{2}\sum_{i=1}^{r}X_{i}(x_{t})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{t},

where the vector fields satisfy Hörmander’s rank condition (Theorem
1.2
), then, formally the conditional expected density of particles is described by the hypoelliptic Anderson equation,

\partial_{t}u-\bar{\mathfrak{L}}u:=\partial_{t}u-\sum_{i=1}^{r}X^{2}_{i}u=u\xi\ .

(1.5)

When realisations of the environment are sufficiently singular then a pathwise solution theory for (
1.5
) is expected to require renormalisation. Since the vector fields
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
satisfy Hörmander’s condition, the operator
\bar{\mathfrak{L}}
is smoothing and therefore in principle, an extension of the theory of regularity structures should be applicable to find a renormalised, pathwise, solution theory for (
1.5
). In Section
6
we apply the analytic tools developed in the paper to demonstrate such an extension to the case where
\bar{\mathfrak{L}}
is the sub-Laplacian on a compact quotient of stratified Lie group (Carnot group).

We show a result analogous to those of
[
Hai14
,
HL18
]
, finding a notion of renormalised solution to (
1.5
), when
\xi
is a coloured periodic noise on a stratified Lie group. More precisely, we show that when
\xi
is replaced with a mollified, recentred noise
\xi_{\varepsilon}-c_{\varepsilon}
, for (specific) diverging constants
\{c_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}
, solutions
u_{\varepsilon}
to (
1.5
) converge in probability to a unique limit independent of the specific choice of mollification-scheme. We stress that this result does not cover the full subcritical regime of (
1.5
). The treatment of this full regime would require an analogue of the BPHZ theorem on homogeneous Lie groups, see
[
CH16
,
HS24
]
.

A notable example of a stratified Lie group is the Heisenberg group,
\mathbb{H}^{n}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2n}\times\mathbb{R}
, see Section
2.4.1
for a description. In this case the collection of left-translation invariant vector fields, which generate the associated Lie algebra are,

A_{i}(x,y,z)=\partial_{x_{i}}+y_{i}\partial_{z},\quad B_{i}(x,y,z)=\partial_{y_{i}}-x_{i}\partial_{z},\quad\text{for }\,i=1,\ldots,n.

It is readily checked that the collections
\{A_{i},B_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are left-translation invariant with respect to the group action described in Section
2.4.1
and that one has
C(x,y,z):=[A_{i},B_{i}]=-2\partial_{z}
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
. The associated sub-Laplacian is the linear differential operator,

\bar{\mathfrak{L}}u=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(A_{i}^{2}+B_{i}^{2})u,

naturally extended to a heat type operator as in (
1.2
) and (
1.5
), c.f. Section
4.4.1

for further discussion.
A phenomenon of interest for Anderson equations is that of localisation, the concentration of the solution at large times, taking arbitrarily large values on islands of arbitrarily small size,
[
CM94
,
Kön16
]
. Since one expects the geometry of the underlying domain to have effect on the emergence of this phenomenon, as also noted in
[
BOTW23
]
and since pathwise approaches to the parabolic Anderson model have proved fruitful in studying finer properties of solutions, see e.g.
[
AC15
,
HL15
,
HL18
,
GUZ19
,
Lab19
,
BDM22
]
, it is our hope that the tools developed herein may prove useful in analysing similar equations on more complex domains. The solution theory exposited in Section
6
applies to precisely this example on a compact quotient of the Heisenberg group.

1.3

Structure and Contributions of the Paper

While it is possible to develop a bespoke solution theory for specific hypoelliptic SPDEs, this rapidly becomes technically challenging and computationally heavy, c.f.
[
HZZZ24
]
. Furthermore, for more complicated equations this bespoke approach quickly becomes impossible. Thus, in our view, the overarching contribution of this article is to show that by taking a more abstract and general point of view one can treat a large class of such equations at once in a framework (surprisingly) close to that of
[
Hai14
]
. In addition, this work highlights the dependence on the algebraic structure of the underlying space and thus contributes to the understanding of solution theories in more general settings, see Section
1.4
.

We begin, in Section
2
, with a recap of analysis on homogeneous Lie groups that is sufficient for our purpose. The most important result in this section is Theorem
2.13
which provides a precise intrinsic version of Taylor’s theorem on homogeneous Lie groups and is a sharper version of existing results, see Remark
2.14
.

Section
3
contains the bulk of the paper and establishes an extension of the analytic aspects of regularity structures to the setting of homogeneous Lie groups. In Section
3.1
we introduce the appropriate notion of abstract polynomials on homogeneous Lie groups which are the backbone of much of our analysis.
In Section
3.2
we discuss modelled distributions. In order to prove reconstruction we extend ideas from
[
FH20
]
, constructing a suitable replacement for the test functions,
\rho
and
\varphi
employed therein. This construction avoids the need for a general theory of wavelets or Fourier analysis on homogeneous Lie groups. In a similar vein, the set-up of singular modelled distributions in Section
3.3
, while conceptually similar to that of
[
Hai14
, Sec. 6]
requires a number technical novelties. In particular, the singular hyperplane of
[
Hai14
]
is replaced with a homogeneous Lie-subgroup and thus statements and proofs are adapted to the underlying (non-commutative) structure.
In Section
3.4
we provide a surprisingly compact proof of the Schauder estimates, building on Theorem
2.13
in combination with the setup of Sections
3.1
&
3.2
in order to reduce to estimates already obtained in
[
Hai14
, Sec. 5]
.
The final three sections, Section
3.5
,
3.6
&
3.7
, carry over almost mutatis mutandis from
[
Hai14
]
and are included for the reader’s convenience.

In Section
4
we demonstrate the application of this general theory to semi-linear evolution equations. We again take an abstract point of view and start with a general homogeneous Lie group and only postulate a “time” direction as a one dimensional subspace of its Lie algebra.
Then, we provide an example fixed point theorem for multiplicative stochastic heat equations in this setting. We note that there is no difficulty in extending the general fixed point theorem of
[
Hai14
, Thm. 7.8]
to our setting, we only specialise for the sake of brevity. In Section
4.4
we present examples of singular kernels on homogeneous Lie groups for which our theory applies, such as the Green’s function of heat operators on the Heisenberg group and Kolmogorov type operators associated to inertial systems.

In Section
5
we explicitly describe a regularity structure for treating linear, Anderson-type equations on homogeneous Lie groups. For this specific class of equations it is notable that one may reuse the algebraic structures developed in
[
BHZ19
]
. However, an algebraic question left open by this work is the extension of the results in
[
BHZ19
]
to homogeneous Lie groups in full generality.

Finally, in Section
6
, we employ the theory developed in the rest of the paper to demonstrate a solution theory for Anderson-type equations on a compact quotient
S
of a stratified Lie group
\mathbb{G}
. Since we do not limit ourselves to quotients by normal subgroups, the space
S
may fail be a group and therefore it is not possible to directly regularise the noise in the usual manner. This last obstacle is circumvented by making use of the fact that
S
is a
\mathbb{G}
-homogeneous space allowing us to define an intrinsic regularisation of the noise.

1.4

Open Problems and Wider Context

As discussed above, translation invariant (and for example) parabolic operators on Euclidean domains serve as a starting point from which
non-translation invariant parabolic problems on Euclidean domains as well as parabolic problems on Riemannian manifolds can be studied.
A somewhat parallel progression holds starting from translation invariant operators on homogeneous Lie groups, moving to general Hörmander operators as well as heat type equations on sub-Riemannian manifolds, see
[
Bra14
]
. This parallel progression in PDE analysis leads one to ask, how far the theory of regularity structures can be extended in these directions. The table below gives a schematic presentation of the progress so far, presents some open problems and places this work within the context of the study of subcritical parabolic-type SPDEs. The two rows describe the two parallel progressions outlined above in the context of regularity structures.

Translation invariant operators on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

Non-translation invariant operators on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

On Riemannian manifolds

The works
[
Hai14
,
BHZ19
,
BCCH21
,
CH16
]
present a general and complete picture.

Analytic step in
[
Hai14
]
; aspects of renormalisation addressed in
[
BB21
,
Sin23
]
.

In
[
DDD19
]
2d-PAM is treated. A general account in
[
HS23
]
.

Translation invariant operators on homogeneous Lie groups

General Hörmander operators

On sub-Riemannian manifolds

Analytic theory covered in this work. Renormalisation and convergence by hand.

Open problem A

Open problem B

Each problem in the second row is closely related to its equivalent problem in the first row, we discuss the posed open problems in a little more detail.

•

Open Problem A is expected to be more involved than its counterpart in the Euclidean setting; going from translation invariant operators to non-translation invariant operators by local approximations. In the case of general Hörmander operators, the underlying Lie group structure would in general vary from point to point. An interesting application would be the study of general hypoelliptic Anderson models (
1.5
) where the underling particles perform a general diffusion with generator of the form (
1.3
), c.f. Theorem
1.2
.

•

Open Problem B is motivated by the fact that analogous to the tangent space being an appropriate local approximations of a Riemannian manifold, the non-holonomic tangent space is an appropriate local approximations of a sub-Riemannian manifold and each fibre of the non-holonomic tangent space is a stratified Lie group, c.f.
[
ABB20
]
. Note that in view of Remark
1.3
the difficulties in Problem B are expected to be somewhat complementary to those of Problem A.

Notation:

Given
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
we let
[\alpha]:=\max\{r\in\mathbb{Z}\,:\,r\leq\alpha\}
denote the integer part of
\alpha
. We often write
\lesssim
to mean that an inequality holds up to multiplication by a constant which may change from line to line but is uniform over any stated quantities. In general we reserve the notation
D
for the usual derivative on Euclidean space, and
X,\,Y
for elements of a Lie algebra thought of as vector fields on the associated Lie group, see Section
2
. We will use
|\,\cdot\,|
to denote the size of various quantities; elements of a Lie group, the Haar measure of subsets of the group, the homogeneity of members of the structure space in a regularity structure, traces of linear maps and the absolute value function on
\mathbb{R}
etc. The meaning will usually be clear from the context but in cases where it is not we will make sure to specify in the text. For integrals we will use the standard notation,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
, for integration against the Haar measure on a given homogeneous, Lie group, see Proposition
2.1
. Throughout most of the article we take an intrinsic point of view and do not equip the homogeneous Lie group with an explicit chart.

2

Analysis on Homogeneous Lie Groups

We collect some basic facts on homogeneous Lie groups and smooth function on them. Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra and
\mathbb{G}
be the unique, up to Lie algebra isomorphism, corresponding simply connected Lie group.
We write
[\cdot,\cdot]
for its Lie bracket and inductively set,
\mathfrak{g}_{(1)}=\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{(n)}=[\mathfrak{g}_{(n-1)},\mathfrak{g}]
. Recalling the exponential map
\exp:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}
, we have the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula

\exp(X)\exp(Y)=\exp(\operatorname{H}(X,Y))\ ,

(2.1)

where
\operatorname{H}
is given by
\operatorname{H}(X,Y)=X+Y+\frac{1}{2}[X,Y]+...
with the remaining terms consisting of higher order iterated commutators of
X
and
Y
; crucially
\operatorname{H}
is universal, i.e. it does not depend on the underlying Lie algebra.

Proposition 2.1

(
[
FS82
, Prop. 1.2]
)
.

Assume that
\mathfrak{g}
is nilpotent, i.e.
\mathfrak{g}_{(n)}={0}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, the exponential map is a diffeomorphism and

•

through this identification of
\mathfrak{g}
with
\mathbb{G}
, the map

\mathbb{G}\times\mathbb{G}\ni(x,y)\mapsto xy\in\mathbb{G}

becomes a polynomial map (between vector spaces).

•

the pull-back to
\mathbb{G}
of the Lebesgue measure on
\mathfrak{g}
is a bi-invariant Haar measure on
\mathbb{G}
.
1
1

1

Recall that a bi-invariant Haar measure on a locally compact Hausdorff group is any Borel measure which is invariant under push-forward by both left and right translates of the underlying group. See the proof of
[
FS82
, Prop. 1.2 (c)]
for further details.

Definition 2.1
.

A dilation on
\mathfrak{g}
is a group of algebra automorphisms
\{\operatorname{D}_{r}\}_{r>0}
of the form
\operatorname{D}_{r}X=\exp(\log r\cdot\mathfrak{s})X
with
\mathfrak{s}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
being a diagonalisable linear operator with
1
as its smallest eigenvalue.

Remark 2.2
.

The requirement that the smallest eigenvalue of
\mathfrak{s}
be
1
is purely cosmetic. Otherwise, denoting the smallest eigenvalue by
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
, one can work with the new operator
\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}\mathfrak{s}
.

Remark 2.3
.

For
a\in\mathbb{R}
, denote by
W_{a}\subset\mathfrak{g}
the eigenspace of
\mathfrak{s}
with eigenvalue
a
. Thus for
X\in W_{a}
,
Y\in W_{b}
one has the condition

\operatorname{D}_{r}[X,Y]=[\operatorname{D}_{r}X,\operatorname{D}_{r}Y]=r^{a+b}[X,Y]

which in particular implies
[W_{a},W_{b}]\subset W_{a+b}
and since
W_{a}=\{0\}
for
a<1

\mathfrak{g}_{(j)}\subset\bigoplus_{a\geq j}W_{a}\ .

In particular, if
\mathfrak{g}
admits a family of dilations, it is nilpotent. The converse is not necessarily true.

Definition 2.2
.

A homogeneous Lie group
\mathbb{G}
is a simply connected Lie group where its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is nilpotent and endowed with a family of dilations
\{\operatorname{D}_{r}\}_{r>0}
. For
r>0
, we define the group automorphism

x\mapsto r\cdot x:=\exp\circ\operatorname{D}_{r}\circ\exp^{-1}x\ .

A homogeneous norm on
\mathbb{G}
is a continuous function
|\cdot|:\mathbb{G}\to[0,\infty)
satisfying the following properties for all
x\in\mathbb{G}
,
r\geq 0

1.

|x|=0
if and only if
x=e
, the neutral element,

2.

|x|=|x^{-1}|\ ,

3.

|r\cdot x|=r|x|\ .

The homogeneous norm naturally induces a topology generated by the open sets and in turn a Borel
\sigma
-algebra. From now on, we will always assume that
\mathbb{G}
is equipped with this topology and
\sigma
-algebra. Furthermore, given a homogeneous group
\mathbb{G}
we denote by
\{X_{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}\in\mathfrak{g}
a basis of eigenvectors of
\mathfrak{s}
with eigenvalues
1=\mathfrak{s}_{1}\leq\mathfrak{s}_{2}\leq...\leq\mathfrak{s}_{d}
and such that

\mathfrak{s}X_{j}=\mathfrak{s}_{j}X_{j}\ .

(2.2)

Given a measurable subset
E\subset\mathbb{G}
we write
|E|
for its Haar measure which we assume to be normalized such that the set
B_{1}=:\{x\in\mathbb{G}\ :\ |x|\leq 1\}
has measure
1
, c.f. Proposition
2.1
. In integrals we use the standard notation
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
.
We define
|\mathfrak{s}|:=\text{trace}(\mathfrak{s})
as the homogeneous dimension of
\mathbb{G}
, since for any measurable subset
E\subset\mathbb{G}
and
r>0
, one has

|r\cdot E|=r^{|\mathfrak{s}|}|E|\ .

We also define balls of radius
r>0
,

B_{r}(x):=\{y\in\mathbb{G}\ :\ |x^{-1}y|<r\}\ .

The topology induced by these balls agrees with the topology of
\mathbb{G}
as a Lie group, see
[
FR16
, Sec. 3.1.6]
. Note that due to the non-commutativity of
\mathbb{G}
, in general
|x^{-1}y|\neq|yx^{-1}|
. We consistently work with the following choice of a semi-metric on the group,

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{G}}(x,y):=|x^{-1}y|=|y^{-1}x|\ .

For
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{G}
we write
\bar{\mathfrak{K}}:=\{z\in\mathbb{G}\,:\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{G}}(z,\mathfrak{K}):=\inf_{y\in\mathfrak{K}}|y^{-1}z|\leq 1\}
for the
1
-fattening of
\mathfrak{K}
.

Remark 2.4
.

A function
f:\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is called homogeneous of degree
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, if
f(r\cdot x)=r^{\lambda}f(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{G}
. One can show, c.f.
[
FS82
, Prop. 1.5 & Prop. 1.6]
, that for any homogeneous norm on
\mathbb{G}
there exists
\mu>0
such that

•

|xy|\leq\mu(|x|+|y|)
for any
x,y\in\mathbb{G}

•

\left||xy|-|x|\right|\leq\mu|y|
for any
x,y\in\mathbb{G}
such that
|y|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x|
.

Furthermore all homogeneous norms are mutually equivalent and we may always choose a homogeneous norm that is smooth away from
e\in\mathbb{G}
,
[
FR16
, Sec. 3.1.6]
.

2.1

Derivatives and Polynomials

We identify
\mathfrak{g}
with the left invariant vector fields
\mathfrak{g}_{L}
on
\mathbb{G}
and write
\mathfrak{g}_{R}
for the right invariant vector fields. We write
X_{i}
for the the basis elements as in (
2.2
) seen as elements of
\mathfrak{g}_{L}
and
Y_{i}
for the basis of
\mathfrak{g}_{R}
satisfying
Y_{i}|_{e}=X_{i}|_{e}
. Thus we can write

X_{j}f(y)=\partial_{t}f(y\exp(tX_{j}))|_{t=0}\quad\text{ and }\quad Y_{j}f(y)=\partial_{t}f(\exp(tY_{j})y)|_{t=0}\

for any smooth function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
.

A map
P:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}
is called a polynomial if
P\circ\exp:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a polynomial on
\mathfrak{g}
.
2
2

2

Recall that the space of polynomial functions on
\mathfrak{g}
is canonically isomorphic to
\bigoplus_{n}(\mathfrak{g}^{\ast})^{\otimes_{s}n}
where
\otimes_{s}
denotes the symmetric tensor product.
Let
\zeta_{i}
be the basis dual to the basis
X_{i}
of
\mathfrak{g}
. We set

\eta_{j}\coloneqq\zeta_{j}\circ\exp^{-1}:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R},

(2.3)

Note that
\eta=(\eta_{1},...,\eta_{d})
forms a global coordinate system
3
3

3

Occasionally we use the corresponding notation
\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta_{i}}
.
and furthermore any polynomial map on
\mathbb{G}
can be written in terms of coefficients
a_{I}\in\mathbb{R}
as

P=\sum_{I}a_{I}\eta^{I}

with the sum running over a finite subset of
\mathbb{N}^{d}
and where for a multi-index
I=(i_{1},...,i_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
we write

\eta^{I}=\eta_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdot...\cdot\eta_{d}^{i_{d}}
.
Define
d(I)=\sum_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}i_{j}
and
|I|=\sum_{j}i_{j}
, we call
\max\{d(I)\ :\ a_{I}\neq 0\}
the homogeneous degree and
\max\{|I|\ :\ a_{I}\neq 0\}
the isotropic degree of
P
. For
a>0
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{a}
the space of polynomials of homogeneous degree strictly less than
a
and define
\triangle=\{d(I)\in\mathbb{R}\ :\ I\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\}
.
4
4

4

We point out a possibly counter-intuitive quirk of our definition; for
k\in\triangle
the set
\mathcal{P}_{k}
does
not
contain polynomials of degree
k
but only those of degree less than
k
.

We can rewrite the group law on
\mathbb{G}
explicitly in terms of
\eta=(\eta_{1},...,\eta_{d})
.

Proposition 2.5
.

For
j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
and multi-indices
I,\,J
s.t.
d(I)+d(J)=\mathfrak{s}_{j}
, there exist constants
C^{I,J}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the following formula holds,

\eta_{j}(xy)=\eta_{j}(x)+\eta_{j}(y)+\sum_{I,J\neq 0,\ d(I)+d(J)=\mathfrak{s}_{j}}C_{j}^{I,J}\eta^{I}(x)\eta^{J}(y)\ ,

for all
x,\,y\in\mathbb{G}
, where
\eta_{j}
is given by the expression (
2.3
).

Proof.

Applying the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula (
2.1
) there exist constants
C^{I,J}_{j}
which depend only on the choice of basis for
\mathfrak{g}
made in (
2.2
) but are independent of
x,\,\&y\in\mathbb{G}
such that

\eta_{j}(xy)=\eta_{j}(x)+\eta_{j}(y)+\sum_{|I|+|J|\geq 2}C^{I,J}_{j}\eta^{I}(x)\eta^{J}(y)\ ,\qquad\text{for all }x,\,y\in\mathbb{G}.

By setting either
x=e
or
y=e
, we find that
C^{I,J}_{j}=0
if
I=0
or
J=0
.
Since furthermore,

\eta_{j}((rx)(ry))=r^{\mathfrak{s}_{j}}\eta_{j}(xy)
the claim follows.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

Proposition
2.5
implies that for
\mathfrak{s}_{j}<2
one has
\eta_{j}(xy)=\eta_{j}(x)+\eta_{j}(y)
while for
\mathfrak{s}_{j}=2
one has
\eta_{j}(xy)=\eta_{j}(x)+\eta_{j}(y)+\sum_{\mathfrak{s}_{k}=\mathfrak{s}_{l}=1}C_{j}^{k,l}\eta_{k}(x)\eta_{l}(y)
.

Remark 2.7
.

It is also noteworthy to realise that Proposition
2.5
implies that
\mathcal{P}_{a}
is invariant under right and left-translations. (This is not true if one replaces homogeneous degree by isotropic degree, except if
\mathbb{G}
is abelian or
a=0
).

Remark 2.8
.

If follows from Proposition
2.5
that one can write

\eta^{K}(xy)=\eta^{K}(x)+\eta^{K}(y)+\sum_{I,J\neq 0,\ d(I)+d(J)=d(K)}C_{K}^{I,J}\eta^{I}(x)\eta^{J}(y)\ ,

where the constants
C_{K}^{I,J}
can be written in terms of the constants
C_{j}^{I,J}
.

Lemma 2.9
.

For
i,\,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
,

X_{i}\eta_{j}=\delta_{i,j}+\sum_{I\neq 0,\ d(I)=\mathfrak{s}_{j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}}C_{j}^{I,e_{i}}\eta^{I}\ ,

(2.4)

where
e_{i}
denotes the multi-index
(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)
with the
1
being in the
i
-th slot.

Proof.

This follows directly from Proposition
2.5
, applying
X_{i}
to the function

y\mapsto\eta_{j}(xy)\ ,

evaluating at
y=0
and using the fact that
X_{i}|_{0}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta_{i}}|_{0}
.
∎

Proposition 2.10

(
[
FS82
, Prop. 1.26]
)
.

One has
X_{j}=\sum P_{j,k}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta_{k}}\right)
where

P_{j,k}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{if }k=j\\
0&\mbox{ if }\mathfrak{s}_{k}\leq\mathfrak{s}_{j},k\neq j\end{array}\right.

and
P_{j,k}
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
\mathfrak{s}_{k}-\mathfrak{s}_{j}
if
\mathfrak{s}_{k}>\mathfrak{s}_{j}
. The analogous statement holds for the vector fields
Y_{j}
,

For a multi-index
I=(i_{1},...,i_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
we introduce the notation
X^{I}=X_{1}^{i_{1}}...X_{d}^{i_{d}}
. Note that the order of the composition matters since
\mathfrak{g}
is not in general Abelian. It is a well known fact that any left invariant differential operator on
\mathbb{G}
can (uniquely) be written as a linear combination of
\{X^{I}\}_{I\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}
. The next proposition follows as a direct consequence.

Proposition 2.11

(
[
FS82
, Prop. 1.30]
)
.

The following maps from
\mathcal{P}_{a}\to\mathbb{R}^{\dim\mathcal{P}_{a}}
are linear isomorphisms.

1.

\,\,P\mapsto\left\{\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial_{\eta}}\right)^{I}P(e)\right\}_{d(I)<a}
,

2.

\,\,P\mapsto\left\{X^{I}P(e)\right\}_{d(I)<a}
,

3.

\,\,P\mapsto\left\{Y^{I}P(e)\right\}_{d(I)<a}
,

The same holds replacing
e\in\mathbb{G}
with any other point
x\in\mathbb{G}
.

Definition 2.3
.

For a smooth function
f:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}
, a point
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
a\in(0,\infty)
, we define the left Taylor polynomial of homogeneous degree (less than)
a
of
f
at
x
to be the unique polynomial
\operatorname{P}^{a}_{x}[f]\in\mathcal{P}_{a}
such that
X^{I}\operatorname{P}^{a}_{x}[f](e)=X^{I}f(x)
for all
I
such that
d(I)<a
. The right Taylor Polynomial can be defined similarly, replacing
X^{I}
by
Y^{I}
.

Remark 2.12
.

One observes that for
I\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
and
a>d(I)
one has that

X^{I}\operatorname{P}^{a}_{x}[f]=\operatorname{P}^{a-d(I)}_{x}[X^{I}f]

for all
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
. Indeed, this follows from the fact that for any
d(J)<a-d(I)
one has
X^{J}(X^{I}\operatorname{P}^{a}_{x}[f])(e)=X^{J}(X^{I}f)(x)
, since
X^{J}X^{I}
can be written as a linear combination of
\{X^{K}\}_{d(K)<a}
.

Theorem 2.13

(Taylor’s Theorem)
.

For each
a>0
and every
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
it holds that

f(xy)-\operatorname{P}^{a}_{x}[f](y)=\sum_{|I|\leq[a]+1,d(I)\geq a}\int_{\mathbb{G}}X^{I}f(xz)Q^{I}(y,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z)\ ,

where for each multi-index
I
and
y\in\mathbb{G}
the measure
Q^{I}(y,\,\cdot\,)
is supported on
B_{\beta^{[a]+1}|y|}(e)
for some
\beta>0
depending only on
\mathbb{G}
and satisfies
\int_{\mathbb{G}}|Q^{I}(y,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z)|\lesssim|y|^{d(I)}
.

Remark 2.14
.

Note that there is a more common version of Taylor’s Theorem on homogeneous Lie groups, c.f.
[
FS82
, Thm. 1.37]
, which states that the remainder satisfies the estimate

|f(xy)-\operatorname{P}^{a}_{x}[f](y)|\lesssim_{a}\sum_{|I|\leq[a]+1,d(I)\geq a}|y|^{d(I)}\sup_{|z|\leq\beta^{[a]+1}|y|}|X^{I}f(xz)|\

(2.5)

and which follows as a trivial consequence of Theorem
2.13
. For us the explicit formula of the reminder is needed in order establish Schauder estimates in Section
3.4
.

Remark 2.15
.

The analogous claim for the right Taylor polynomial,
(\operatorname{P}_{R})^{a}_{x}[f]
, holds. In this case the analogue of (
2.5
) reads

|f(yx)-(\operatorname{P}_{R})^{a}_{x}[f](y)|\lesssim_{a}\sum_{|I|\leq[a]+1,d(I)\geq a}|y|^{d(I)}\sup_{|z|\leq\beta^{[a]+1}|y|}|Y^{I}f(zx)|\ .

(2.6)

Remark 2.16
.

If we define
\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{a}_{x}[f](y):=\operatorname{P}^{a}_{x}[f](x^{-1}y)
, then it follows that

f(y)-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{a}_{x}[f](y)=\sum_{|I|\leq[a]+1,d(I)\geq a}\int_{\mathbb{G}}X^{I}f(xz)Q^{I}(x^{-1}y,dz)

and in particular that

|f(y)-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{a}_{x}[f](y)|\lesssim_{a}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|I|\leq[a]+1\\
d(I)\geq a\end{subarray}}|x^{-1}y|^{d(I)}\sup_{|z|\leq C|x^{-1}y|}|X^{I}f(xz)|\ .

In the particular case when
f
is compactly supported, one can rewrite this for the rescaled function
f^{\lambda}(x)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{|\mathfrak{s}|}}f(\lambda\cdot x)
as

\displaystyle|f^{\lambda}(y)-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{a}_{x}[f^{\lambda}](y)|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{a}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|I|\leq[a]+1\\
d(I)\geq a\end{subarray}}\lambda^{-d(I)-|\mathfrak{s}|}|x^{-1}y|^{d(I)}\sup_{z\in\mathbb{G}}|X^{I}f(z)|

(2.7)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{a,f}\sum_{\delta\geq 0}\lambda^{-(a+\delta)-|\mathfrak{s}|}|x^{-1}y|^{a+\delta}\ ,

where the sum over
\delta
runs over some finite subset of
[0,\infty)
.

Remark 2.17
.

Given
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
, if we define
F\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}\times\mathbb{G})
by setting
F(y,z)=f(z^{-1}y)
, we find that

\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{a}_{x}[F(\,\cdot\,,z)](y)=\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{a}_{z^{-1}x}[f](z^{-1}y)\ .

Remark 2.18
.

For any
p\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
one has

\tilde{\operatorname{P}}_{x}^{\gamma}[p](y)=p(y)\ .

Remark 2.19
.

In the proof of Theorem
2.13
, given below, we use the following elementary observations

•

The map

\displaystyle\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{G},

\displaystyle(t_{1},...,t_{d})

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp(t_{1}X_{1})\cdot...\cdot\exp(t_{d}X_{d})

is a diffeomorphism. To see this note that it is clearly a local diffeomorphism since one has

\displaystyle D\Phi|_{0}:T\mathbb{R}^{d}|_{0}\sim\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\to T\mathbb{G}|_{0}\sim\mathfrak{g}

\displaystyle(t_{1},...,t_{d})

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i}t_{i}X_{i}.

Then since,

\Phi(r^{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}t_{1},...,r^{\mathfrak{s}_{d}}t_{d})=r\cdot\Phi(t_{1},...,t_{d})

it is seen to be a global diffeomorphism.

•

Setting for
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

|t|_{\mathfrak{s}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}|t_{i}|^{1/\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\ ,

one finds that there exists some
\beta=\beta(\mathbb{G})>0
such that

\frac{1}{\beta}|t|_{\mathfrak{s}}\lesssim|\Phi(t)|\lesssim\beta|t|_{\mathfrak{s}}

(2.8)

uniformly over
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

•

By repeated use of the commutator, for any
I,J\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
there exist coefficients
\lambda_{i,J,I}
such that

X_{i}X^{J}=\sum_{I}\lambda_{i,J,I}X^{I}

(2.9)

and one has
\lambda_{i,J,I}=0
whenever
d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}\neq d(I)
.

•

If
a\in\triangle
, then
m_{a}:=\max\{|I|\ :\ d(I)\leq a\}=[a]
where
[a]
denotes the integer part of
a
. This follows from the fact that
d(I)\leq a
implies
m_{a}\leq[a]
and since
([a],0,...,0)
is in the set over which the maximum is taken.

Proof of Theorem
2.13
.

Define for
i\in\{1,...,d\}
the following measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with support on
B^{\mathfrak{s}}_{|t|_{\mathfrak{s}}}(0)

\tilde{Q}^{e_{i}}(t,ds)=\prod_{j<i}\delta_{t_{j}}(ds_{j})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{[0,t_{i}]}(ds_{i})\prod_{j>i}\delta_{0}(ds_{j})

and define

{Q}^{e_{i}}(y,\,\cdot\,)=\Phi_{*}\tilde{Q}^{e_{i}}(\Phi^{-1}(y),\,\cdot\,)
to be the push-forward measure. First we show that,

f(xy)-f(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{G}}(X_{i}f)(xz){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)\ .

(2.10)

Indeed one can write for
y=y_{1}y_{2}\ldots y_{d}
, where
y_{i}=\exp(t_{i}X_{i})
and
t=(t_{1},...,t_{d})=\Phi^{-1}(y)

\displaystyle f(xy)-f(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}f(xy_{1}\ldots y_{i-1}y_{i})-f(xy_{1}...y_{i-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{t_{i}}\partial_{s}f(xy_{1}\ldots y_{i-1}\exp(sX_{i}))|_{s=s^{\prime}}ds^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{t_{i}}(X_{i}f)(xy_{1}\ldots y_{i-1}\exp(sX_{i}))ds

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(X_{i}f)(x\Phi(s))\tilde{Q}^{e_{i}}(t,ds)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{G}}(X_{i}f)(xz){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)\ .

Using the fact that
\Phi
is a diffeomorphism one easily checks that
{Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)
is supported on
B_{\beta|y|}(0)
where
\beta
is as in (
2.8
), and satisfies
\int_{\mathbb{G}}|Q^{e_{i}}|(y,dz)\lesssim|y|^{d_{i}}
, thus we have proved the theorem in the special case
a\in(0,1]
, also known as mean-value theorem.
We turn to the proof in the general case.

Claim 2.20
.

Set
g(y)=f(xy)-\operatorname{P}^{a}_{x}[f](y)
, we note that
X^{J}g(e)=0
for
d(J)<a
while
X^{J}g(y)=X^{J}f(xy)
for
d(J)\geq a
.
We shall prove that for any multi-index
J
it holds that one can write

X^{J}g(y)=\sum_{|I|\leq[a]+1,d(I)\geq a}\int_{\mathbb{G}}X^{I}f(xz)Q^{I,J}(y,dz)\ ,

where the measures
Q^{I,J}(x,\cdot)
satisfy the following properties

•

Q^{I,J}(x,\cdot)
is supported on
B_{\beta^{m(J)}|y|}(e)\subset\mathbb{G}
where
m(J)=\min\{m\in\mathbb{N}:\ a-m<d(J)\}\ ,

•

\int_{\mathbb{G}}|Q^{I,J}|(x,dy)\lesssim|x|^{d(I)-d(J)}
.

We shall prove by induction on
m\in\{1,...,\lceil a\rceil\}
, that for multi-indices
J
satisfying
a-m\leq d(J)<a
the claim holds.

•

The case
m=1
follows from (
2.9
) and (
2.10
),

\displaystyle X^{J}g(y)=X^{J}g(y)-X^{J}g(e)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{G}}(X_{i}X^{J}g)(z){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\sum_{I\ :\ d(I)=d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\lambda_{i,J,I}(X^{I}g)(z){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\sum_{I\ :\ d(I)=d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\lambda_{i,J,I}(X^{I}f)(xz){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|I|\leq[a]+1,d(I)\geq a}\int_{\mathbb{G}}X^{I}f(xz)Q^{I,J}(y,dz)

The properties of
Q^{I,J}(y,dz)
follow from the corresponding properties of
{Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)
, completing the case
m=1
.

•

Suppose the claim holds for
m-1
. Then by the same argument as above

\displaystyle X^{J}g(y)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{d(K)=d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\lambda_{i,J,K}\int_{\mathbb{G}}(X^{K}g)(xz){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,K:d(K)=d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}<a}\lambda_{i,J,K}\int_{\mathbb{G}}(X^{K}g)(xz){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i,K:d(K)=d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}\geq a}\lambda_{i,J,K}\int_{\mathbb{G}}(X^{K}g)(xz){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz).

We can apply the induction hypothesis to the terms in the first sum, since
d(K)=d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}\geq d(J)+1\geq a-(m-1)
and find

(X^{K}g)(xz)=\sum_{|I|\leq[a]+1,d(I)\geq a}\int_{\mathbb{G}}X^{I}f(x\tilde{z})Q^{I,K}(z,d\tilde{z})

and therefore

\displaystyle X^{J}g(y)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,K:d(K)=d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}<a}\int_{\mathbb{G}}(X^{K}g)(xz){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i,K:d(K)=d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}\geq a}\lambda_{i,J,K}\int_{\mathbb{G}}(X^{K}g)(xz){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,K:d(K)=d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}<a}\lambda_{i,J,K}\sum_{|I|\leq k+1,d(I)\geq a}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\int_{\mathbb{G}}X^{I}f(x\tilde{z})Q^{I,K}(z,d\tilde{z}){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i,K:d(K)=d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}\geq a}\lambda_{i,J,K}\int_{\mathbb{G}}(X^{K}g)(xz){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz).

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,K:d(K)=d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}<a}\lambda_{i,J,K}\sum_{|I|\leq k+1,d(I)\geq a}\int_{G}X^{I}f(x\tilde{z})\big(Q^{I,K}*{Q}^{e_{i}}\big)(y,d\tilde{z})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i,K:d(K)=d(J)+\mathfrak{s}_{i}\geq a}\lambda_{i,J,K}\int_{\mathbb{G}}(X^{K}g)(xz){Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{|I|\leq[a]+1,d(I)\geq a}\int_{\mathbb{G}}X^{I}f(x\tilde{z})Q^{I,J}(y,d\tilde{z})\,

where
Q^{I,J}
is defined such that the last line holds true. Concerning the measures
Q^{I,J}(y,\cdot)
, we note that

1.

\supp Q^{I,J}(y,\cdot)\subset B_{\beta^{m(J)}|y|}
since

\supp\big(Q^{I,K}*{Q}^{e_{i}}(y,\cdot)\big)\subset B_{\beta^{m(K)+1}|y|}

as
\supp Q^{I,K}(y,\cdot)\subset B_{\beta^{m(K)}|y|}(e)
and
\supp{Q}^{e_{i}}(y,dz)\subset B_{\beta|y|}(e)
.

2.

\int_{\mathbb{G}}|Q^{I,J}(y,\cdot)|\lesssim|x|^{d(I)-d(J)}
since

\int|Q^{I,K}*{Q}^{e_{i}}|(y,dz)\leq\int|Q^{I,K}|(y,dz)\,\cdot\,\int|{Q}^{e_{i}}|(y,dz)\lesssim|y|^{d(I)-d(K)}\cdot|y|^{\mathfrak{s}_{i}}\ .

This completes the proof.
∎

2.2

Distributions and Convolution

We define
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G}):=C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G})
to be the space of compactly supported smooth functions on
\mathbb{G}
equipped with the canonical LF-topology. The space of distributions on
\mathbb{G}
is given by the dual
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{G})
of
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
and for
\xi\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{G})
and
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
we either write
\langle\phi,\xi\rangle
or
\xi(\phi)
for the canonical pairing.
Given
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
we introduce the following useful space of test functions

\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}_{r}(x):=\left\{\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{\lambda}(x))\ :\ |X^{I}\phi|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{|\mathfrak{s}|+d(I)}}\ \forall I:d(I)\leq r\right\}\ .

We will often use the shorthand
\mathfrak{B}_{r}:=\mathfrak{B}^{1}_{r}(e)
.
Given
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
we extensively use the following notations,

\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(z):=\frac{1}{\lambda^{|\mathfrak{s}|}}\phi\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot(x^{-1}z)\right).

Recalling that we use the notation
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
for the Haar measure on
\mathbb{G}
we define the norms,

\|f\|_{L^{p}}:=\begin{cases}\left(\int_{\mathbb{G}}|f(x)|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/p},&\text{ for }1\leq p<\infty,\\
\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{G}}|f(x)|,&p=\infty.\end{cases}

By left and right translation invariance of the Haar measure, for
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{G})
it holds that

\int_{\mathbb{G}}f(yx)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{G}}f(xy)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{G}}f(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathbb{G}}f(y^{-1})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.

See the discussion after
[
FR16
, Thm. 1.1.1]
.

Remark 2.21
.

From the scaling properties of
\operatorname{D}
it follows that

\mathfrak{B}^{\lambda}_{r}(x)=\left\{\phi^{\lambda}_{x}\ :\phi\in\mathfrak{B}^{1}_{r}(e)\right\}.

One defines the convolution of two functions
\phi,\psi:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\psi*\phi(x)=\int\psi(y)\phi(y^{-1}x)dy=\int\psi(xy^{-1})\phi(y)dy\ .

(2.11)

Note that in general
\psi*\phi(x)\neq\phi*\psi(x)
but still many of the usual inequalities hold, in particular the Young inequality, c.f.
[
FS82
, Prop. 1.18]
. One can write

\psi*\phi(x)=\int\psi(y)\phi_{y}(x)dy\ .

We extend convolution to generalised function, i.e. elements of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{G})
, by duality in the usual manner.
From now on we will use the notation
\tilde{\phi}(z):=\phi(z^{-1})
, one notes that the following identities hold

\displaystyle\langle f,g*\phi\rangle=\langle\tilde{g}*

\displaystyle f,\phi\rangle=\langle f*\tilde{\phi},g\rangle

(2.12)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f*g}

\displaystyle=\tilde{g}*\tilde{f}

(2.13)

\displaystyle\psi^{\lambda}*\phi^{\lambda}=(\psi*\phi)^{\lambda}\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad(\psi\ast\phi)_{x}=\psi_{x}\ast\phi.

Recalling the notation
X^{I}=X_{1}^{i_{1}}...X_{d}^{i_{d}}
for any multi-index
I=(i_{1},...,i_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, we introduce the analogous notation

Y^{I}=Y_{d}^{i_{d}}....Y_{1}^{i_{1}}

for the right invariant basis vectors. Note the reversal in the composition. Since for any right invariant vector field
Y
and left invariant vector field
X
such that
X|_{e}=Y|_{e}
it holds that
\langle Xf,g\rangle=\langle f,Yg\rangle
for
f,\,g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G})
, it follows from the definition of the convolution that

•

X^{I}(\psi*\phi)=\psi*(X^{I}\phi)
,

•

Y^{I}(\psi*\phi)=(Y^{I}\psi)*\phi
,

•

(X^{I}\psi)*\phi=\psi*Y^{I}\phi\
.

Definition 2.4
.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
we define the space
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{G})
of
\alpha
-Holder continuous distributions to be those
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{G})
such that for every compact set
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{G}
,

•

If
\alpha\leq 0

\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{K})}=\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{K}}\sup_{\phi\in\mathfrak{B}_{\lceil{-\alpha\rceil}}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1)}\frac{|\langle f,\phi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|}{\lambda^{\alpha}}<+\infty\ .

•

If
\alpha>0
, there exists a continuous map
\mathbb{G}\to\mathcal{P}_{\alpha},\ x\mapsto\tilde{P}_{x}
such that

\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{K})}=\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{K}}\sup_{\phi\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1)}\frac{|\langle f-\tilde{P}_{x},\phi_{x}^{\lambda}\rangle|}{\lambda^{\alpha}}+\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{K}}|\tilde{P}_{x}|_{\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}}<+\infty\ ,

(2.14)

where
|\,\cdot\,|_{\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}}
denotes any norm on the finite dimensional vector space
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}
.

Furthermore,
C(\mathbb{G})
denotes the space of continuous functions (note that only the strict inclusion
C(\mathbb{G})\subsetneq\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{G})
holds).

Remark 2.22
.

We note that as in
[
Hai14
]
, for
\alpha\in\triangle
the
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{G})
spaces do not agree with the usual notion of continuously
\alpha
-differentiable functions. This definition, however, is more canonical in the context of regularity structures, c.f. Theorem
3.12
.

The following proposition gives some useful properties that also mirror those of Euclidean Hölder spaces.

Proposition 2.23
.

Given any real number
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
the following holds.

1.

\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{G})\subset\mathcal{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{G})
for every
\beta<\alpha
.

2.

For any multiindex
I
the map
X^{I}:\ \mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{G})\to\mathcal{C}^{\alpha-d(I)}(\mathbb{G}),\quad f\mapsto X^{I}f
is well defined.

3.

If
\alpha>0
, then any distribution
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{G})
agrees
5
5

5

As usual, we say a distribution agrees with a continuous functions, if it lies in the image of the canonical embedding
C(\mathbb{G})\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{G})
.
with a continuous function.

Note that this proposition in particular implies that
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})=\cap_{\alpha>0}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{G})
.

Proof.

Points
1
and
2
follow directly. For Point
3
, denote by
\tilde{P}_{x}
the polynomial in Definition
2.4
, we set
F=f-\tilde{P}_{\cdot}(\cdot)
, then for any
\psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{0}
such that
\int_{\mathbb{G}}\psi=c^{-1}>0
and smooth compactly supported function
g
we find

\displaystyle\langle F,g\rangle=c\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\langle F,g*\psi^{\lambda}\rangle=c\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\langle F,\int_{\mathbb{G}}g(y)\psi_{y}^{\lambda}dy\right\rangle=c\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\langle F,\psi_{y}^{\lambda}\rangle g(y)dy=0\ ,

where in the last step we used that since
y\to\tilde{P}_{y}
is continuous we have

\langle F,\psi_{y}^{\lambda}\rangle=\langle f-\tilde{P}_{y}(\,\cdot\,),\psi_{y}^{\lambda}\rangle+\int\big(\tilde{P}_{y}(z)-\tilde{P}_{z}(z)\big)\psi_{y}^{\lambda}(z)\,dz\to 0\ ,

as
\lambda\to 0
.
Thus we find that the distribution
f
agrees with the continuous function
x\mapsto\tilde{P}_{x}(x)
, concluding the proof.
∎

Remark 2.24
.

Note that by Proposition
2.23
for
\alpha>0
and any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{G})
we know that
X^{I}f
agrees with a continuous function whenever
d(I)<\alpha
. Thus, in particular Definition
2.3
of Taylor polynomials
\operatorname{P}^{\alpha}_{x}[f]
extends to
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{G})\supset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
.

2.3

Discrete Subgroups

Recall that, given a discrete subgroup
\mathfrak{G}\subset\mathbb{G}
acting on
\mathbb{G}
(say) on the left, then the quotient space
S:=\mathbb{G}/\mathfrak{G}=\{\mathfrak{G}x\ :\ x\in\mathbb{G}\}
is a smooth manifold. Furthermore, the quotient map
\pi:\mathbb{G}\to S
is a smooth normal covering map , c.f.
[
Lee13
, Thm. 21.29]
and the canonical
\mathbb{G}
right action

S\times\mathbb{G}\to S,\qquad(\mathfrak{G}x,x^{\prime})\mapsto\mathfrak{G}xx^{\prime}\

makes it a homogeneous space.
We call
\mathfrak{G}
a
lattice
if
S=\mathbb{G}/\mathfrak{G}
is a compact space. This is equivalent to
S
carrying a finite
\mathbb{G}
invariant measure, which we shall denote by
d(\mathfrak{G}x)
, c.f.
[
Rag07
, Thm. 2.1]
. Throughout this article we assume that every homogeneous Lie group we work with carries a lattice, the following theorem,
[
Rag07
, Thm. 2.12]
, gives sufficient conditions for this to be the case, which all our examples satisfy.

Theorem 2.25
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a simply connected nilpotent Lie group and
\mathfrak{g}
its Lie algebra. Then,
\mathbb{G}
admits a lattice if and only if
\mathfrak{g}
admits a basis with respect to which the structure constants of
\mathfrak{g}
are rational.

Let us point out that there do exist nilpotent Lie groups that do not admit a basis with respect to which the structure constants are rational, see
[
Rag07
, Rem. 2.14]
for an example.

Denote by
\pi^{\ast}:C(S)\to C(\mathbb{G})
the pull-back under
\pi
. We define a convolution map

*_{S}:C^{\infty}(S)\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\to C^{\infty}(S),\quad(f,\phi)\mapsto f*_{S}\phi

as the unique map, such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(S)\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\times C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\ ,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\ast}\times\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}

where convolution on the bottom row is convolution on the group as defined in
2.11
.
In order to check that this map is well defined, we use the following terminology. A function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is called left
\mathfrak{G}
periodic, if for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
n\in\mathfrak{G}
it holds that

f(x)=f(nx)\ .

Thus, we only need to check that for any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
and any left
\mathfrak{G}
periodic function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
, the function

f*\phi
is left
\mathfrak{G}
periodic. Indeed for any
n\in\mathfrak{G}
and
x\in\mathbb{G}
, it holds that

\displaystyle f*\phi(nx)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}}f(y)\phi(y^{-1}nx)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}}f(y)\phi((n^{-1}y)^{-1}x)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}}f(ny)\phi(y^{-1}x)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}}f(y)\phi(y^{-1}x)dy\ .

By duality, one naturally extends the notion of convolution on
S
to pairs,
(\xi,\zeta)\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(S)\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{c}(\mathbb{G})
, where
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{c}(\mathbb{G})
denotes distributions on
\mathbb{G}
with compact support.

2.4

Concrete Examples

In order to cement ideas we present two concrete examples of non-abelian, homogeneous Lie groups, both of which are identified with fixed global charts. These will be revisited in Section
4.4
when we discuss the heat operator on the Heisenberg group and Kolmogorov type operators.

2.4.1

The Heisenberg group

Given
n\geq 1
we define the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{n}
as the set
\mathbb{R}^{2n}\times\mathbb{R}
with the group law,

(x,y,z)(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})=\left(x+x^{\prime},y+y^{\prime},z+z^{\prime}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(x^{\prime}_{i}y_{i}-x_{i}y^{\prime}_{i}\right)\right).

Remark 2.26
.

Note that one may equally define the complex Heisenberg group on
\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
equipped with the group law,

(u,z)(u^{\prime},z^{\prime})=\left(u+u^{\prime},z+z^{\prime}+\Im\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}\bar{u}^{\prime}_{i}\right),

where
\Im z
denotes the imaginary part. One sees that these definitions are equivalent after identifying
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.

The origin
e=(0,0,0)
is clearly the identity and for
(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}\times\mathbb{R}
one has
(x,y,z)^{-1}=(-x,-y,-z)
. The Lie algebra,
\mathfrak{h}
of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
is identified with
\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
and spanned by the basis of left-invariant vector fields,

A_{i}(x,y,z)=\partial_{x_{i}}+y_{i}\partial_{z},\quad B_{i}(x,y,z)=\partial_{y_{i}}-x_{i}\partial_{z},\quad C(x,y,z)=\partial_{z}

and equipped with the Lie bracket
[A,B]=AB-BA
. We observe that for any
(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
and
i=1,\ldots,n
,

[A_{i},B_{i}](x,y,z)=-2C.

(2.15)

We say that a Lie algebra is graded if there exist vector spaces
\{W_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
where only finitely many
W_{k}
are non-zero,
\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{\infty}W_{k}
and
[W_{i},W_{j}]\subset W_{i+j}
.

Definition 2.5
.

A homogeneous Lie group
\mathbb{G}
is called stratified (or a Carnot group) if its Lie algebra is graded and generated by
W_{1}
.

Due to (
2.15
), we see that if we equip
\mathbb{H}^{n}
with the dilation map,

\lambda\cdot(x,y,z):=(\lambda x,\lambda y,\lambda^{2}z),

then
\mathbb{H}^{n}
becomes a stratified group. Refer to
[
Bra14
, Sec. 3.3.6]
for a discussion of generalisations of this structure.

A simple example of a lattice on the Heisenberg group is the set of integer vectors
(a,b,c)\in\mathfrak{H}^{n}:=\mathbb{Z}^{2n}\times\mathbb{Z}
equipped with the group law as defined above. Note that it is not a normal subgroup and thus the quotient space
\mathbb{H}^{n}/\mathfrak{H}^{n}
is not a group. However, since it is a lattice the homogeneous space
\mathbb{H}^{n}/\mathfrak{H}^{n}
is compact, c.f. Subsection
2.3
.

2.4.2

Matrix Exponential Groups

For
n\geq 1
, let
B
be a rational
n\times n
block matrix of the form,

B=\begin{pmatrix}0&B_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&B_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&0&B_{k}\\
0&0&\cdots&0&0\end{pmatrix}

with each
B_{i}
a
p_{i-1}\times p_{i}
block matrix of rank
p_{i}
, where
n\geq p_{0}\geq p_{1}\geq\cdots\geq p_{k}
and
\sum_{i=0}^{k}p_{i}=n
. Note that this implies the zero blocks on the diagonal are all
p_{i}\times p_{i}
square matrices. We can equip
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
with an associated Lie structure by defining the group law

(t,z)(s,z^{\prime}):=\left(t+s,z^{\prime}+\exp\left(sB^{\top}\right)z\right).

To define the dilation we begin by decomposing according to the structure of the block matrix
B
, writing

\mathbb{R}^{n}=\mathbb{R}^{p_{0}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{p_{n}}.

Thus the action of
B^{\top}
on
z=(z_{0},...,z_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{0}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{p_{k}}
is written
B^{\top}z=(B^{\top}_{1}z_{0},\,B^{\top}_{2}z_{1},...B^{\top}_{k}z_{k-1})
etc. Then we set

\lambda\cdot(t,z_{0},\ldots,z_{k}):=(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda z_{0},\ldots,\lambda^{2k+1}z_{k}).

The origin
e=(0,0)
is again the identity element and
(t,z)^{-1}=(-t,-\exp(-tB^{\top})z)
. The Lie algebra is spanned by the translation invariant vector fields,

X_{i}(t,z)=\partial_{z_{i}}\quad\text{for }i=1,\ldots,p_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad Y(t,z)=\partial_{t}-(Bz)\cdot\nabla=\partial_{t}-\sum_{i,j=p_{0}+1}^{n}b_{ij}z_{i}\partial_{z_{i}}.

This defines the matrix exponential group associated to
B
and one sees that it is not stratified.

The simplest non-trivial example is to set
n=2
and

B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},

so that using the suggestive notation
(t,v,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
, the group law becomes,

(t,v,x)(s,w,y)=(t+s,v+w,x+y+sv).

The equivalent scaling as above is to set
\lambda\cdot(t,x,v)=(\lambda^{2}t,\lambda v,\lambda^{3}x)
. We refer to
[
Man97
]
for more details and Section
4.4
below for a discussion of natural second order hypoelliptic linear operators associated to these groups.

3

Regularity Structures and Models

Definition 3.1
.

A regularity structure is a pair
\mathcal{T}=(\operatorname{T},\operatorname{G})
consisting of the following elements:

1.

A graded vector space
\operatorname{T}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in A}\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}
where

•

the index set
A\subset\mathbb{R}
is discrete, bounded from below and contains zero,

•

each
\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}
is finite dimensional with a fixed norm
|\cdot|_{\alpha}
. We write
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha}:\operatorname{T}\to\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}
for the canonical projection,

•

\operatorname{T}_{0}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
with a distinguished element
\mathbf{1}\in\operatorname{T}_{0}
, such that
|\mathbf{1}|_{0}=1
.

The space
\operatorname{T}
is called the structure space.

2.

A group
\operatorname{G}
of linear operators acting on
\operatorname{T}
, such that for every
\Gamma\in\operatorname{G}
it holds that
\Gamma|_{\operatorname{T}_{0}}
is the identity map and for all
\tau\in\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}
:

\Gamma\tau-\tau\in\bigoplus_{\beta<\alpha}\operatorname{T}_{\beta}.

The group
\operatorname{G}
is called the structure group of
\,\mathcal{T}
.

A sector is a
\operatorname{G}
invariant subspace
V\subset\operatorname{T}
and if
V\neq\{0\}
the regularity of the sector
V
is defined as
\min\{\alpha\in A\ :\ V\cap\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}\neq\{0\}\}
.

We make use of the natural shorthands,
\operatorname{T}_{>\alpha},\,\operatorname{T}_{\geq\alpha},\,\operatorname{T}_{<\alpha},\,\operatorname{T}_{\leq\alpha}
and the projections
\mathcal{Q}_{>\alpha}
etc.

Definition 3.2
.

Given a regularity structure
\mathcal{T}=(\operatorname{T},\operatorname{G})
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that
r>|\min A|
, a model for
\mathcal{T}
is a pair
M=(\Pi,\Gamma)
, consisting of

•

a realisation map
\Pi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to L(T,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{G})),\ x\mapsto\Pi_{x}
, such that for any compact set
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{G}
one has
\|\Pi\|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}:=\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{K}}\|\Pi\|_{\gamma,x}<+\infty
for all
\gamma>0
, where

\|\Pi\|_{\gamma,x}:=\sup_{\zeta\in A\cap(-\infty,\gamma)}\sup_{\tau\in T_{\zeta}}\sup_{\lambda<1}\sup_{\phi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}}\frac{|\langle\Pi_{x}\tau,\phi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|}{|\tau|_{\zeta}\lambda^{\zeta}},

•

a re-expansion map
\Gamma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\operatorname{G},\ (x,y)\mapsto\Gamma_{x,y}
, which satisfies the algebraic conditions

\Pi_{x}\Gamma_{x,y}=\Pi_{y},\qquad\Gamma_{x,y}\Gamma_{y,z}=\Gamma_{x,z}

and the analytic condition
\|\Gamma\|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}:=\sup_{x,y\in\mathfrak{K}:\ {|y^{-1}x|}<1}\|\Gamma\|_{x,y,\gamma}<+\infty
for all
\gamma>0
,
where

\|\Gamma\|_{x,y,\gamma}:=\sup_{\zeta\in A\cap(-\infty,\gamma)}\sup_{A\ni\beta<\zeta}\sup_{\tau\in T_{\zeta}}\frac{|\Gamma_{x,y}\tau|_{\beta}}{{|y^{-1}x|}^{\zeta-\beta}|\tau|_{\zeta}}.

Lastly, we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{T}}
the space of models for
\mathcal{T}
equipped with the semi-norms
\|M\|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}:=\|\Pi\|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}+\|\Gamma\|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}
.

Remark 3.1
.

In the rest of the article, often without any further comment, we will write

r:=\min\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}\ :\ n>|\min A|\right\}\ .

3.1

The Polynomial Regularity Structure

As an important example we describe the polynomial regularity structure and canonical model which are crucial in the analysis of singular SPDEs on homogeneous Lie groups. We define the structure space
\bar{\operatorname{T}}
to be the symmetric tensor (Hopf) algebra generated by
\{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
, which we think of as abstract lifts of the monomials
\{\eta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
. For a multi-index
I
, we write
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{I}:=\boldsymbol{\eta}_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdot...\cdot\boldsymbol{\eta}_{d}^{i_{d}}
as well as
\mathbf{1}:=\boldsymbol{\eta}^{0}
. The group
\bar{\operatorname{G}}
is given by a copy of
\mathbb{G}
acting by

g\mapsto\Big(\Gamma_{g}:\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}+\sum_{I,J\neq 0,\ d(I)+d(J)=\mathfrak{s}_{j}}C_{j}^{I,J}\eta^{I}(g)\boldsymbol{\eta}^{J}\ +\eta_{j}(g)\mathbf{1}\Big)

and
\Gamma_{g}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{I}=\big(\Gamma_{g}\boldsymbol{\eta}\big)^{I}
.
The canonical polynomial model is defined by setting

\Pi_{x}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}(z)=\eta_{j}(x^{-1}z)

and

\Gamma_{x,y}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}=\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}+\sum_{I,J\neq 0,\ d(I)+d(J)=\mathfrak{s}_{j}}C_{j}^{I,J}\eta^{I}(y^{-1}x)\boldsymbol{\eta}^{J}\ +\eta_{j}(y^{-1}x)\mathbf{1}\ .

These maps are extended multiplicatively to all of
\bar{\operatorname{T}}
.
Using Prop.
2.5
in the third equality,

\displaystyle\Pi_{x}\Gamma_{x,y}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}(z)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{x}\Big(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}+\sum_{I,J\neq 0,\ d(I)+d(J)=\mathfrak{s}_{j}}C_{j}^{I,J}\eta^{I}(y^{-1}x)\boldsymbol{\eta}^{J}\ +\eta_{j}(y^{-1}x)\Big)(z)

\displaystyle=\eta_{j}(x^{-1}z)+\sum_{I,J\neq 0,\ d(I)+d(J)=\mathfrak{s}_{j}}C_{j}^{I,J}\eta^{I}(y^{-1}x)\eta^{J}(x^{-1}z)\ +\eta_{j}(y^{-1}x)

\displaystyle=\eta_{j}(y^{-1}xx^{-1}z)

\displaystyle=\eta_{j}(y^{-1}z)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{y}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}(z).

3.1.1

Derivatives and abstract Polynomials

Next we lift the vector fields
X_{i}
to abstract differential operators
\mathcal{X}^{i}
on
\bar{\operatorname{T}}
of degree
\mathfrak{s}_{j}
, c.f. Section
3.5
. We set

\mathcal{X}^{i}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{j}=\delta_{i,j}\mathbf{1}+\sum_{I\neq 0,\ d(I)=\mathfrak{s}_{j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}}C_{j}^{I,e_{i}}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{I}

and extend this definition to the whole regularity structure by the Leibniz rule.
The conditions to be an abstract differential operator are checked directly:

1.

Indeed
\mathcal{X}^{i}:\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}\mapsto T_{\alpha-\mathfrak{s}_{i}}
.

2.

By the Leibinz rule the second property is checked by showing that

\mathcal{X}^{i}\Gamma_{x,y}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{j}=\Gamma_{x,y}\mathcal{X}^{i}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{j}\ .

(3.1)

Note that
\Pi_{x}\mathcal{X}^{i}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{j}=X_{i}\Pi_{x}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{j}\ ,
since

\Pi_{x}\mathcal{X}^{i}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}(z)=\Pi_{x}\Big(\delta_{i,j}\mathbf{1}\,+\hskip-1.99997pt\sum_{I\neq 0,\ d(I)=\mathfrak{s}_{j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}}C_{j}^{I,e_{i}}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{I}\Big)=\delta_{i,j}\,+\hskip-1.99997pt\sum_{I\neq 0,\ d(I)=\mathfrak{s}_{j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}}C_{j}^{I,e_{i}}\eta^{I}(x^{-1}z)

and using left invariance of the vector field
X_{i}
as well as (
2.4
)

X_{i}\Pi_{x}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{j}(z)=(X_{i}\eta_{j}(x^{-1}\cdot))(z)=(X_{i}\eta_{j})(x^{-1}z)=\delta_{i,j}+\sum_{I\neq 0,\ d(I)=\mathfrak{s}_{j}-\mathfrak{s}_{i}}C_{j}^{I,e_{i}}\eta^{I}(x^{-1}z)\ .

Thus (
3.1
) follows from the injectivity of the maps
\Pi_{x}
and the relation
\Pi_{x}\Gamma_{x,y}=\Pi_{y}
.

Remark 3.2
.

In addition to showing that
\mathcal{X}_{i}
is an abstract differential operator, we have shown that it also realizes
X_{i}
for the polynomial model.

3.1.2

Abstract Taylor Expansions

The following operator, which sends a smooth function to its abstract Taylor expansion, will be used throughout the article.

Definition 3.3
.

For
x\in\mathbb{G}
, we define the family of maps

\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{a}_{x}:\mathcal{C}^{a}(\mathbb{G})\to\bar{\operatorname{T}}

where for
a>0

\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{a}_{x}[f]
is characterised by the fact that
\Pi_{e}\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{a}_{x}[f]=\operatorname{P}^{a}_{x}[f]\in\mathcal{P}_{a}
and for
a\leq 0
it satisfies
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{a}_{x}[f]=0
.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that the map
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{a}_{x}
is well defined by Remark
2.24
. We shall almost exclusively use it, when its argument is a smooth function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\subset\mathcal{C}^{a}(\mathbb{G})
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
and
a\geq 0
. Then for each multi-index
I
the coefficient of
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{I}
in
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{a}_{x}[f]
is given by a linear combination (depending only on
\mathbb{G}
) of
\{X^{K}f(x)\}_{d(K)\leq d(I)}
. Furthermore, for
b\geq a
one has

\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}_{x}^{a}[f]=\mathcal{Q}_{<a}\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{b}_{x}[f]\ .

(3.2)

Proof.

We first observe that (
3.2
) holds since the polynomial
\Pi_{e}\mathcal{Q}_{\leq a}\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{b}_{x}[f]
satisfies the property required in Definition
2.3
. The first part of the lemma follows by combining Proposition
2.11
with (
3.2
). ∎

Lemma 3.5
.

In the setting of the above lemma one has

|X^{I}\Pi_{e}\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{a}_{x}[f](z)|=|X^{I}\operatorname{P}^{a}_{x}[f](z)|\lesssim\sum_{d(I)\leq d(J)<a}\sup_{d(J^{\prime})\leq d(J)}|X^{J^{\prime}}f(x)||z|^{d(J)-d(I)}\ .

Proof.

Since the coefficient of
{\eta}^{J}
in
{\operatorname{P}}^{a}_{x}[f]
is bounded by a universal multiple of
\sup_{d(J^{\prime})\leq d(J)}|X^{J^{\prime}}f(x)|
by Lemma
3.4
the claim follows using the fact that

|X^{I}\Pi_{e}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{J}(z)|\lesssim|z|^{{d(J)-d(I)}}
.
∎

The next lemma will be useful when proving Schauder estimates in Section
3.4
, since it allows us to circumvent the issue of having non-explicit Taylor polynomials.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\delta>0
and
P\in\bar{\operatorname{T}}_{<\delta}
. If, for some
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
,

\left|(X^{I}\Pi_{e}P)(e)\right|\leq\varepsilon^{\delta-d(I)}

for all
I\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
such that
\delta>d(I)
,
then it holds that
|P|_{a}\lesssim_{\delta,\mathbb{G}}\varepsilon^{\delta-a}
for all
a\leq\delta
.

Proof.

We observe that
\operatorname{P}_{x}^{\delta}[\Pi_{e}P]=\Pi_{e}P
. Thus, the claim follows directly from Lemma
3.4
.
∎

3.2

Modelled Distributions

Definition 3.4
.

Given a regularity structure
\mathcal{T}=(\operatorname{T},\operatorname{G})
, a model
M=(\Pi,\Gamma)
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
we define
\mathscr{D}_{M}^{\gamma}
as the space of all continuous maps
f:\mathbb{G}\to\operatorname{T}_{<\gamma}
, such that for all
\zeta\in A\cap(-\infty,\gamma)
the following bounds hold for every compact set
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{G}

\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{K}}|f(x)|_{\zeta}<+\infty,\qquad\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathfrak{K},\\
0<{|y^{-1}x|}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{|f(y)-\Gamma_{x,y}f(x)|_{\zeta}}{{|y^{-1}x|}^{\gamma-\zeta}}<+\infty\ .

We define the corresponding semi-norm
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}

on
\mathscr{D}_{M}^{\gamma}
by setting,

\|f\|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}:=\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{K}}\sup_{\zeta<\gamma}|f(x)|_{\zeta}+\sup_{\zeta<\gamma}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathfrak{K},\\
0<{|y^{-1}x|}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{|f(y)-\Gamma_{x,y}f(x)|_{\zeta}}{{|y^{-1}x|}^{\gamma-\zeta}}.

Given two models
M=(\Pi,\Gamma),\,\bar{M}=(\bar{\Pi},\bar{\Gamma})
, two modelled distributions
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}_{M},\,\bar{f}\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}_{\bar{M}}
and a compact set
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{G}
we define the quantity,

\displaystyle\|f;\bar{f}\|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}:=

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{K}}\sup_{\zeta<\gamma}|f(x)-\bar{f}(x)|_{\zeta}

\displaystyle+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathfrak{K}\\
0<{|y^{-1}x|}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sup_{\zeta<\gamma}\frac{|f(y)-\bar{f}(y)-\Gamma_{x,y}f(y)+\bar{\Gamma}_{x,y}\bar{f}(x)|_{\zeta}}{{|y^{-1}x|}^{\gamma-\zeta}}.

For the set of modelled distributions taking values in a sector
V\subset\operatorname{T}
we write
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}_{M}(V)
and if the regularity of the sector is
\alpha\in A
we often use the shorthand
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}_{\alpha;M}
. We will freely drop the explicit dependence on the model, image sector and its regularity when the context is clear.

3.2.1

Reconstruction

Theorem 3.7

(Reconstruction Theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}=(\operatorname{T},\operatorname{G})
be a regularity structure with
\underline{\alpha}=\min A
. Then for every
\gamma>0
and
M=(\Pi,\Gamma)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{T}}
, there exists a unique, continuous linear map
\mathcal{R}_{M}:\mathscr{D}_{M}^{\gamma}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\underline{\alpha}}
, called the reconstruction operator associated to
M
, such that for any compact
\mathfrak{K}
and
\lambda\in(0,1]

\sup_{\psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}}|\langle\mathcal{R}_{M}f-\Pi_{x}f(x),\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|\lesssim_{\mathfrak{K}}\lambda^{\gamma}\|f\|_{\gamma;B_{2\mu\lambda}(x)}\|\Pi\|_{\gamma;B_{2\mu\lambda}(x)}\ ,

(3.3)

uniformly over
x\in\mathfrak{K}
, where
\mu\geq 1
was defined in Remark
2.4
.
Furthermore the map
M\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{M}
is locally Lipschitz continuous in the sense that for a second model
\bar{M}=(\bar{\Pi},\bar{\Gamma})
and
\bar{f}\in\mathscr{D}_{\bar{M}}^{\gamma}
, for every
\lambda\in(0,1]

\displaystyle\sup_{\psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}}|\langle\mathcal{R}_{M}f-\mathcal{R}_{\bar{M}}\bar{f}-\Pi_{x}f(x)+\bar{\Pi}_{x}\bar{f}(x),\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|\lesssim_{\mathfrak{K}}\lambda^{\gamma}\bigg(

\displaystyle\|f;\bar{f}\|_{\gamma;B_{2\mu\lambda}(x)}\|\bar{\Pi}\|_{\gamma;B_{2\mu\lambda}(x)}

(3.4)

\displaystyle+\|f\|_{\gamma;B_{2\mu\lambda}(x)}\|\Pi-\bar{\Pi}\|_{\gamma;B_{2\mu\lambda}(x)}\bigg)\ ,

Remark 3.8
.

Existence of a reconstruction operator for
\gamma\leq 0
also holds, however, uniqueness does not. In the case
\gamma=0
the analogous bound to (
3.3
) contains an additional logarithmic correction on the right hand side, c.f.
[
CZ20
]
.

Remark 3.9
.

It follows from the definition of the reconstruction operator, that if
f
is a modelled distributions with values in a sector
V\subset\operatorname{T}
of regularity
\alpha\geq\underline{\alpha}
, then
\mathcal{R}_{M}f\in C^{\alpha}
.

In order to prove Theorem
3.7
we require two preliminary results. As in
[
FH20
, Sec. 13.4]
we make the observation that for every
N\geq 0
there exists a
\rho:\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, smooth and compactly supported in
B_{1}(e)
and such that

\int\eta^{I}(x)\rho(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\delta_{I,0},\quad 0<d(I)\leq N,

(3.5)

where the
\delta
here denotes the Kronecker delta applied componentwise to the multi-index. For
\mathfrak{r}>1
we define
\rho^{(n)}(x):=\mathfrak{r}^{n|\mathfrak{s}|}\rho(\mathfrak{r}^{n}\cdot x)
as well as,

\rho^{(n,m)}=\rho^{(n)}\ast\rho^{(n+1)}\ast\cdots\ast\rho^{(m)},

with the convention
\rho^{(n,n)}=\rho^{(n)}
. We then have the following result.

Lemma 3.10
.

If
\,\mathfrak{r}>\|\rho\|_{L^{1}}>1
, then there exists a smooth, compactly supported function
\varphi^{(n)}=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\rho^{(n,m)}
, where the convergence takes place in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
and
\supp(\varphi^{(n)})\subset B_{C\mathfrak{r}^{-n}}
for
C=\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{\mathfrak{r}-1}
.

Proof.

First, note that since
\|\rho^{(m)}\|_{L^{1}}=\|\rho\|_{L^{1}}
and
\rho^{(m)}
is supported on a ball of radius
\mathfrak{r}^{-m}
, it follows from the mean value theorem on
\mathbb{G}
(c.f. (
2.5
) with
a=0
) that

|f\ast\rho^{(m)}(x)-f(x)|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{G}}(f(y)-f(x))\rho^{(m)}(y^{-1}x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\right|\lesssim\max_{i=1,...,d}\|Y_{i}f\|_{{L^{\infty}}}\|\rho\|_{L^{1}}\mathfrak{r}^{-m}\ .

Secondly, since

Y^{I}\rho^{(n,m)}=Y^{I}(\rho^{(n)}\ast\rho^{(n+1,m)})=(Y^{I}\rho^{(n)})\ast\rho^{(n+1,m)}

(3.6)

one finds by applying Young’s convolution inequality
m
-times, c.f. Section
2.2
, that

\displaystyle\|Y_{i}\rho^{(n,m)}\|_{L^{\infty}}=\|(Y_{i}\rho^{(n)})\ast\rho^{(n+1,m)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|Y_{i}\rho^{(n)}\|_{\infty}\|\rho\|_{L^{1}}^{m-n-1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{r}^{n(\mathfrak{s}_{i}+|\mathfrak{s}|)}\|Y_{i}\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\rho\|^{m-n-1}_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{r}^{2n|\mathfrak{s}|}\|Y_{i}\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\rho\|^{m-n-1}_{L^{1}}

and therefore

\displaystyle\|\rho^{(n,m)}-\rho^{(n,m-1)}\|_{L^{\infty}}=\|\rho^{(n,m-1)}\ast\rho^{(m)}-\rho^{(n,m-1)}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{i=1,...,d}\|Y_{i}\rho^{(n,m-1)}\|_{{L^{\infty}}}\|\rho\|_{L^{1}}\mathfrak{r}^{-m}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{r}^{2n|\mathfrak{s}|}\max_{i=1,...,d}\|Y_{i}\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\rho\|^{m-n-2}_{L^{1}}\mathfrak{r}^{-m}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{r}^{2n|\mathfrak{s}|}\frac{\max_{i=1,...,d}\|Y_{i}\rho\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\|\rho\|^{n+2}_{L^{1}}}\left(\frac{\|\rho\|_{L^{1}}}{\mathfrak{r}}\right)^{m}

which is summable in
m
since we assumed that
\mathfrak{r}>\|\rho\|_{L^{1}}
. Thus we may write,

\rho^{(n,m)}=\rho^{(n)}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-n-1}\rho^{(n,m-k)}-\rho^{(n,m-1-k)},

and it follows that
\rho^{(n,m)}
converges uniformly as
m\to+\infty
. Using (
3.6
) we obtain convergence in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
.
It remains to check the support of
\varphi^{(n)}
. For two functions
f_{1},f_{2}
such that
\supp(f_{i})\in B_{r_{i}}
one has

\supp(f_{1}\ast f_{2})\in B_{r_{1}+r_{2}}
, hence it follows that
\varphi^{(n)}
is supported in a ball of radius
\sum_{m=n}^{\infty}\mathfrak{r}^{-m}=\frac{\mathfrak{r}^{-n}}{1-\mathfrak{r}^{-1}}
.
∎

It follows from the definitions that

\varphi^{(n)}=\rho^{(n)}\ast\varphi^{(n+1)}
. We set

\tilde{\rho}^{(m,n)}:=\tilde{\rho}^{(m)}\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(m-1)}\ast\cdots\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(n)}

and using (
2.13
) we note that
\tilde{\varphi}^{(m+1)}\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(m)}=\tilde{\varphi}^{(m)}
and
\tilde{\rho}^{(m,n)}\to\tilde{\varphi}^{(n)}
in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G})
as
m\to\infty
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{r}>1
and
\rho
be as in Lemma
3.10
Let
\alpha>0
and
\xi_{n}:\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a sequence of functions such that for every compact
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{G}
there exists a
C_{\mathfrak{K}}
such that
\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{K}}|\xi_{n}(x)|\leq C_{\mathfrak{K}}\mathfrak{r}^{\alpha n}
and such that
\xi_{n}=\xi_{n+1}\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(n)}
. Then the sequence
\xi_{n}
is Cauchy in
\mathcal{C}^{-\beta}(\mathbb{G})
for every
\beta>\alpha
and the limit
\xi
satisfies
\xi_{n}=\xi\ast\tilde{\varphi}^{(n)}
.
If furthermore, for some
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
\gamma>-\alpha
one has the bound

|\xi_{n}(y)|\leq\mathfrak{r}^{\alpha n}\left(|x^{-1}y|^{\gamma+\alpha}+\mathfrak{r}^{-(\gamma+\alpha)n}\right)

uniformly over
n\geq 0
and
y\in\mathbb{G}
such that
|x^{-1}y|\leq 1
, then
|\langle\xi,\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|\lesssim\lambda^{\gamma}
for all
\lambda\leq 1
and
\phi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}
, where
r=-[-\alpha]
.

Proof.

The proof follows along the same steps as that of
[
FH20
, Lem. 13.24]
, but one has to be careful since convolution is non-commutative in our setting. Let
\lambda\in(0,1]
and
\psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}
, we first establish the bound

|\langle\xi_{n}-\xi_{n+1},\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|\lesssim\lambda^{-\beta}\mathfrak{r}^{(\alpha-\beta)n}

(3.7)

uniformly over
\psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}
,
\lambda\in(0,1]
and locally uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{G}
. First observe the trivial bound,

|\langle\xi_{n}-\xi_{n+1},\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|\leq\sup_{x\in\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}\left(|\xi_{n}(x)|+|\xi_{n+1}(x)|\right)\|\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\|_{L^{1}}\leq(1+\mathfrak{r}^{\alpha})C_{\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}\bar{C}\mathfrak{r}^{\alpha n},

where
