\bar{C}:=\sup\{\int|\psi^{\lambda}(x)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\ :\ \psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}\}<+\infty
. Hence, when
\lambda\leq\mathfrak{r}^{-n}
the bound (
3.7
) holds directly.

In the case
\mathfrak{r}^{-n}\leq\lambda
, using (
2.12
) we rewrite

|\langle\xi_{n}-\xi_{n+1},\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|=\langle\xi_{n+1}\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(n)}-\xi_{n+1},\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|=|\langle\xi_{n+1},\psi_{x}^{\lambda}*\rho^{(n)}-\psi_{x}^{\lambda}\rangle|.

By Taylor’s theorem and in particular Remark
2.16

|\psi_{x}^{\lambda}(z)-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{r}_{y}[\psi_{x}^{\lambda}](z)|\,\lesssim_{r}\sum_{\delta>0}\lambda^{-(r+\delta)-|\mathfrak{s}|}|y^{-1}z|^{r+\delta}\ ,

(3.8)

where the sum runs over a finite set. It follows from (
2.11
), Remark
2.8
, Remark
2.16
and (
3.5
) that we have

\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{r}_{y}[\psi^{\lambda}_{x}]*\rho^{(n)}(z)=\int\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{r}_{y}[\psi^{\lambda}_{x}](zw^{-1})\rho^{(n)}(w)dw=\int\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{0}_{y}[\psi^{\lambda}_{x}](zw^{-1})\rho^{(n)}(w)dw=\psi_{x}^{\lambda}(y)

and thus

\psi_{x}^{\lambda}*\rho^{(n)}(y)-\psi_{x}^{\lambda}(y)=(\psi_{x}^{\lambda}-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{r}_{y}[\psi^{\lambda}_{x}])\ast\rho^{(n)}(y),

which by (
3.8
) is bounded uniformly by a multiple of
\sum_{\delta>0}\lambda^{-(r+\delta)-|\mathfrak{s}|}\mathfrak{r}^{-n(r+\delta)}\
and supported on a ball of radius
\lambda+\mathfrak{r}^{-n}\leq 2\lambda
. Note that we were able to keep the convolution with
\rho^{(n)}
on the right in both expressions by using the rebased Taylor polynomial of Rem.
2.16
. Using the bound
|\xi_{n+1}|\lesssim_{\alpha}\mathfrak{r}^{\alpha n}
we conclude that

|\langle\xi_{n+1},\psi_{x}^{\lambda}*\rho^{(n)}-\psi_{x}^{\lambda}\rangle|\lesssim\sum_{\delta>0}\lambda^{-(r+\delta)}\mathfrak{r}^{-n(r+\delta)}\mathfrak{r}^{\alpha n}\lesssim\lambda^{-\beta}\mathfrak{r}^{(\alpha-\beta)n}

where we used
\mathfrak{r}^{-n}\leq\lambda
and without loss of generality assumed that
r\geq\beta
in the last line.
Hence
\{\xi_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\mathcal{C}^{-\beta}(\mathbb{G})
and for any test function,

\langle\xi_{n},\psi\rangle=\langle\xi_{n+1},\psi*\rho^{(n)}\rangle=\langle\xi_{m+1},\psi*\rho^{(n,m)}\rangle=\langle\xi,\psi*\varphi^{(n)}\rangle,

showing that
\xi_{n}=\xi*\tilde{\varphi}^{(n)}
.

To prove the second claim, for any test function
\psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}
,
\lambda>0
and
x\in\mathbb{G}
we write,

\langle\xi,\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle=\langle\xi_{n},\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle+\sum_{k\geq n}\langle\xi_{k+1}-\xi_{k},\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle,

where
n
is chosen so that
\lambda\in[\mathfrak{r}^{-(n+1)},\mathfrak{r}^{-n}]
and as a consequence

|\langle\xi_{n},\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|\leq\lambda^{-|\mathfrak{s}|}\mathfrak{r}^{\alpha n}\int_{B_{\lambda}(x)}\left(|x^{-1}y|^{\gamma+\alpha}+\mathfrak{r}^{-(\gamma+\alpha)n}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\,\lesssim\,\lambda^{\gamma+\alpha}\mathfrak{r}^{\alpha n}+\mathfrak{r}^{-\gamma n}\lesssim\lambda^{\gamma}.

To bound the summands
\langle\xi_{k+1}-\xi_{k},\varphi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle
we proceed as in the proof of the first claim to find that

\displaystyle|\langle\xi_{k}-\xi_{k+1},\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle\xi_{k+1},\psi^{\lambda}_{x}*\rho^{(k)}-\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle\xi_{k+1},(\psi^{\lambda}_{x}-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{r}_{x}[\psi^{\lambda}_{x}]))*\rho^{(k)}\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\delta>0}\lambda^{-(r+\delta)-|\mathfrak{s}|}\mathfrak{r}^{-k(r+\delta)}\int_{B_{2\lambda}(x)}|\xi_{k+1}(y)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\delta>0}\lambda^{-(r+\delta)-|\mathfrak{s}|}\mathfrak{r}^{k(\alpha-r-\delta)}\left(\int_{B_{2\lambda}(x)}|x^{-1}y|^{\gamma+\alpha}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y+\mathfrak{r}^{-(\gamma+\alpha)(k+1)}\lambda^{|\mathfrak{s}|}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\delta>0}\left(\lambda^{\gamma+\alpha-r-\delta}\mathfrak{r}^{k(\alpha-r-\delta)}+\lambda^{-(r+\delta)}\mathfrak{r}^{-k(\gamma+r+\delta)}\right)

where the sum in
\delta
is again over a finite set. Since
r+\delta>\alpha
the quantity on the left is summable over
k\geq n
and is of order
\lambda^{\gamma}
, concluding the proof.
∎

We are now ready to proof Theorem
3.7
.

Proof of Theorem
3.7
.

For
m>0
we first define the operators
\mathcal{R}^{(m,m)}:\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}\rightarrow C(\mathbb{G})
by setting,

\left(\mathcal{R}^{(m,m)}f\right)(y):=\left(\Pi_{y}f(y)\ast\tilde{\varphi}^{(m)}\right)(y)=\langle\Pi_{y}f(y),{\varphi}^{(m)}_{y}\rangle.

We then set, for
n<m
,

\mathcal{R}^{(m,n)}f=\mathcal{R}^{(m,m)}f\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(m-1,n)}

and recalling that
\tilde{\varphi}^{(m+1)}\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(m)}=\tilde{\varphi}^{(m)}
we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{(m,n)}f-\mathcal{R}^{(m+1,n)}f

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}^{(m,m)}f\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(m-1,n)}-\mathcal{R}^{(m+1,m+1)}f\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(m,n)}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathcal{R}^{(m,m)}f-\mathcal{R}^{(m+1,m+1)}f\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(m)}\big)\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(m-1,n)}\ .

Using the identity

(F\ast\tilde{\phi})(x)=\langle F,\phi_{x}\rangle=\int F(y)\phi_{x}(y)dy\ ,

it follow by a straightforward computation that

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{R}^{(m,n)}f-\mathcal{R}^{(m+1,n)}f\big)(x)

\displaystyle=\int\big(\mathcal{R}^{(m,m)}f-\mathcal{R}^{(m+1,m+1)}f\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(m)}\big)(y){\rho}_{x}^{(m-1,n)}(y)dy

\displaystyle=\int\int\langle\Pi_{y}f(y)-\Pi_{z}f(z),{\varphi}_{z}^{(m+1)}\rangle{\rho}_{y}^{(m)}(z)dz{\rho}_{x}^{(m-1,n)}(y)dy\ .

Therefore, using the fact that
\Pi_{z}=\Pi_{y}\Gamma_{yz}
, we have,

\big(\mathcal{R}^{(m,n)}f-\mathcal{R}^{(m+1,n)}f\big)(x)=\int\int\langle\Pi_{y}(f(y)-\Gamma_{yz}f(z)),{\varphi}_{z}^{(m+1)}\rangle{\rho}_{y}^{(m)}(z)dz{\rho}_{x}^{(m-1,n)}(y)dy.

Then successively applying the facts that,

•

\sup_{y,\,z\in B_{2\mu\mathfrak{r}^{-m}}(0)}|\langle\Pi_{y}\tau,\varphi^{(m+1)}_{z}|\lesssim\|\Pi\|_{\gamma;B_{2\mu\mathfrak{r}^{-m}}(0)}\mathfrak{r}^{-\alpha m}|\tau|_{\alpha}
for
\tau\in T_{\alpha}
,

•

\|f(y)-\Gamma_{yz}f(z)\|_{\alpha}\lesssim\|f\|_{\gamma;B_{\mathfrak{r}^{-m}}(y)}\mathfrak{r}^{(\alpha-\gamma)m}
uniformly over
|y^{-1}z|\lesssim\mathfrak{r}^{-m}

•

\|\rho_{x}^{(n,m-1)}\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim 1
uniformly over
m>n\geq 0
,

we establish the bound,

\|\mathcal{R}^{(m,n)}f-\mathcal{R}^{(m+1,n)}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{K})}\lesssim\|\Pi\|_{\gamma,\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}\|f\|_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}\mathfrak{r}^{-\gamma m}\ ,

uniformly over
m\geq n\geq 0
, where
\bar{\mathfrak{K}}
denotes the two fattening of the set
\mathfrak{K}
. It follows directly from the definition and properties of a model that we also have the bound,

\|\mathcal{R}^{(n,n)}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{K})}\lesssim\|\Pi\|_{\gamma,\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}\|f\|_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}\mathfrak{r}^{-\underline{\alpha}n},

(3.9)

where
\underline{\alpha}=\min A
. It follows that
\mathcal{R}^{(m,n)}f
converges uniformly on compacts as
m\rightarrow\infty
to
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}f
which also satisfies the bound(
3.9
). Since it also holds that for every
m\geq n+1
,

\mathcal{R}^{(m,n)}f=\mathcal{R}^{(m,m)}f\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(m-1,n)}=\mathcal{R}^{(m,m)}f\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(m-1,n+1)}\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(n)}=\mathcal{R}^{(m,n+1)}f\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(n)}\ ,

we find that

\mathcal{R}^{(n)}f=\mathcal{R}^{(n+1)}f\ast\tilde{\rho}^{(n)}\ .

Therefore we may apply Lemma
3.11
to see that there exists a limit
\mathcal{R}f:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{R}^{(n)}f
.

With validity of the limit established we now turn to show the bounds (
3.3
) and (
3.4
); this requires us to keep more careful track of the underlying sets in the proof. We begin with (
3.3
), first noting that if we define
f_{x}(y):=\Gamma_{y,x}f(x)
then one has
\mathcal{R}^{(n,n)}f_{x}=\Pi_{x}f(x)\ast\tilde{\varphi}^{(n)}
so that
(
3.3
) can be written as the claim that for all
\lambda\in(0,1]
,

\sup_{\psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}}|\langle\mathcal{R}(f-f_{x}),\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|\lesssim_{\mathfrak{K}}\lambda^{\gamma}\|f\|_{\gamma;B_{2\lambda}(x)}\|\Pi\|_{\gamma;B_{2\lambda}(x)}\ .

(3.10)

Using that
|(f-f_{x})(z)|_{\alpha}\lesssim\|f\|_{\gamma;B_{{|z^{-1}x|}}(x)}{|z^{-1}x|}^{\gamma-\alpha}
for
x,\,z\in\mathfrak{K}
, it follows that for all
y\in B_{\lambda}(x)
one has

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{R}^{(n,n)}(f-f_{x})(y)|=|\langle\Pi_{y}(f(y)-f_{x}(y)),\varphi^{(n)}_{y}\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle\Pi_{y}(f(y)-\Gamma_{y,x}f(x)),\varphi^{(n)}_{y}\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Pi\|_{\gamma;B_{\lambda}(y)}\|f\|_{\gamma;B_{C\mathfrak{r}^{-n}}(y)}\sum_{\underline{\alpha}\leq\alpha\leq\gamma}\mathfrak{r}^{-\alpha n}|y^{-1}x|^{\gamma-\alpha}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Pi\|_{\gamma;B_{\lambda}(y)}\|f\|_{\gamma;B_{C\mathfrak{r}^{-n}}(y)}\mathfrak{r}^{-\underline{\alpha}n}({|y^{-1}x|}^{\gamma-\underline{\alpha}}+\mathfrak{r}^{(\underline{\alpha}-\gamma)n}),

(3.11)

where
C(\mathfrak{r}):=\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{\mathfrak{r}-1}
is as in Lemma

3.10
. Given
n>n_{0}(\lambda)
sufficiently larger, we have a uniform bound
C\mathfrak{r}^{-n}\leq\lambda
. By the convergence of
\mathcal{R}^{(n,n)}
in the first exponent, it follows that
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
satisfies the same bound and so inspecting the proof of the second half of Lemma
3.11
, in particular noticing that we integrate the above estimate over
y\in B_{\lambda}(x)
, we conclude that (
3.10
) holds for the limit
\mathcal{R}
.

Using the obvious notation we can also rewrite (
3.4
) as

\displaystyle\sup_{\psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}}|\langle\mathcal{R}(f-f_{x})-\bar{\mathcal{R}}(\bar{f}-\bar{f}_{x}),\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|\lesssim\lambda^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\big(\|f;\bar{f}\|_{\gamma;B_{2\lambda}(x)}\|\bar{\Pi}\|_{\gamma;B_{2\lambda}(x)}

\displaystyle+\|f\|_{\gamma;B_{2\lambda}(x)}\|\Pi-\bar{\Pi}\|_{\gamma;B_{2\lambda}(x)}\big)\ ,

uniformly over
\lambda\in(0,1]
. This is seen very similarly to (
3.10
) but using this time that for
n
large enough,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{R}^{(n,n)}(f-f_{x})-\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{(n,n)}(\bar{f}-\bar{f}_{x})(y)|

\displaystyle=|\langle\Pi_{y}(f(y)-\Gamma_{x,y}f(x))-\bar{\Pi}_{y}(\bar{f}(y)-\bar{\Gamma}_{y,x}\bar{f}(x)),\varphi^{(n)}_{y}\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle\Pi_{y}(f(y)-\Gamma_{x,y}f(x)-\bar{f}(y)+\bar{\Gamma}_{y,x}\bar{f}(x))+(\Pi_{y}-\bar{\Pi}_{y})(\bar{f}(y)-\bar{\Gamma}_{y,x}\bar{f}(x)),\varphi^{(n)}_{y}\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(\|\Pi\|_{\gamma;B_{\lambda}(y)}\|f;\bar{f}\|_{\gamma;B_{\lambda}(y)}+\|\Pi-\bar{\Pi}\|_{\gamma;B_{\lambda}(y)}\|\bar{f}\|_{\gamma;B_{\lambda}(y)}\big)\sum_{\underline{\alpha}\leq\alpha\leq\gamma}\mathfrak{r}^{-\alpha n}{|y^{-1}x|}^{\gamma-\alpha}\ .

It remains to show that the reconstruction map is unique for
\gamma>0
and that
\mathcal{R}f
is indeed an element of
C^{\underline{\alpha}}
. This is done exactly as in
[
Hai14
, Sec. 3]
.
∎

3.2.2

Functions as Modelled Distributions

Let
\bar{\mathcal{T}}=(\bar{\operatorname{T}},\bar{\operatorname{G}})
be the polynomial regularity structure equipped with the polynomial model. We show that in this setting the reconstruction theorem and its inverse map Taylor polynomials to Hölder functions and vice versa.

Theorem 3.12
.

For
\gamma>0
the reconstruction operator is an isomorphism between
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{G})
and
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{G})
.
In particular the inverse of the reconstruction map is given by

\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{G})\ni f(\cdot)\mapsto\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{\gamma}_{(\,\cdot\,)}[f]\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{G}),

where
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{a}
is defined in Definition
3.3

Proof.

Given a modelled distribution in
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}
and since we are working with the polynomial regularity structure equipped with its canonical model, it follows directly from the bound satisfied by the image of the reconstruction operator, i.e. Equation(
3.3
), and the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}
in (
2.14
) that the reconstruction operator is a map
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{G})\to\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{G})
. Continuity also follows from (
3.3
) and by linearity.

To see the other direction recall that given a Hölder continuous distribution
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{G})
by Proposition
2.23
the
\{X^{I}f\}_{d(I)<\gamma}
are actually functions and, in particular, that
\tilde{P}_{x}=\Pi_{x}\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{[\gamma]}_{x}[f]
. Therefore, for
\lambda=|x^{-1}y|_{\mathbb{G}}

\left|\Pi_{x}\big(\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{[\gamma]}_{x}[f]-\Gamma_{x,y}\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{[\gamma]}_{y}[f]\big)(\psi^{\lambda}_{x})\right|=|\langle\tilde{P}_{x}-f,\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|+|\langle f-\tilde{P}_{y},\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|\lesssim\lambda^{\gamma}\

(3.12)

uniformly in
\psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}
.
On the other hand, writing
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{[\gamma]}_{x}[f]-\Gamma_{x,y}\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{[\gamma]}_{y}[f]=\sum_{d(I)<\gamma}c^{I}_{x,y}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{I}
, we find that

\Pi_{x}\big(\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{[\gamma]}_{x}[f]-\Gamma_{x,y}\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{[\gamma]}_{y}[f]\big)(\psi^{\lambda}_{x})=\sum_{d(I)<\gamma}c^{I}_{x,y}\Pi_{e}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{I}(\psi^{\lambda})=\sum_{d(I)<\gamma}c^{I}_{x,y}\lambda^{d(I)}\Pi_{e}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{I}(\psi)\ .

Using that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
is a finite dimensional vector space and the surjectivity of the linear map

C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{1}(e))\to\mathbb{R}^{\dim\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}},\quad\psi\mapsto\{\Pi_{e}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{I}(\psi)\}_{d(I)<\gamma}

we find that

\sum_{d(I)<\gamma}|c^{I}_{x,y}|\lambda^{d(I)}\lesssim_{\gamma}\sup_{\psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}}\left|\sum_{d(I)<\gamma}c^{I}_{x,y}\lambda^{d(I)}\Pi_{e}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{I}(\psi)\right|\ .

(3.13)

Together, (
3.12
) and (
3.13
) imply that
|c^{I}_{x,y}|\lesssim\lambda^{\gamma-d(I)}
, we may then apply Lemma
3.6
to see that
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{[\gamma]}_{(\,\cdot\,)}[f]\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}
.
∎

3.2.3

Local Reconstruction

We will require the following further refinement of the reconstruction theorem which is an analogue in our case of
[
Hai14
, Prop. 7.2]
.

Proposition 3.13
.

In the setting of Theorem
3.7
one has the improved bound,

\sup_{\psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{m}}|(\mathcal{R}f-\Pi_{x}f(x))(\psi^{\lambda}_{x})|\lesssim\lambda^{\gamma}\|\Pi\|_{\gamma;B_{2\mu\lambda}(x)}\sup_{y,\,z\in\supp(\psi^{\lambda}_{x})}\sup_{\ell<\gamma}\frac{|f(z)-\Gamma_{zy}f(y)|_{\ell}}{{|y^{-1}z|}^{\gamma-\ell}}\ ,

(3.14)

as well as, given a second model
\bar{M}(\bar{\Pi},\bar{\Gamma})
and a modelled distribution
\bar{f}\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}_{\bar{M}}
, the analogous bound, that for every
\lambda\in(0,1]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\psi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}}|\langle\mathcal{R}_{M}f-\mathcal{R}_{\bar{M}}\bar{f}-\Pi_{x}f(x)+\bar{\Pi}_{x}\bar{f}(x),\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle|\lesssim

\displaystyle\lambda^{\gamma}\bigg(\|f;\bar{f}\|_{\gamma;\supp(\psi^{\lambda}_{x})}\|\bar{\Pi}\|_{\gamma;B_{2\mu\lambda}(x)}

(3.15)

\displaystyle+\|f\|_{\gamma;\supp(\psi^{\lambda}_{x})}\|\Pi-\bar{\Pi}\|_{\gamma;B_{2\mu\lambda}(x)}\bigg)\ ,

for any
x\in\mathbb{G}
and any
\lambda\in(0,1]
.

Proof.

Since the right hand side of (
3.14
) is linear in
f
, as in
[
Hai14
]
we may assume it to be equal to
1
.
We use the functions
\rho^{(n)}
and
\varphi^{(n)}
from Section
3.2.1
and recall that they satisfy

\varphi^{(n-1)}(x)=\int_{B_{\mathfrak{r}^{-n+1}}}\rho^{(n-1)}(y)\varphi_{y}^{(n)}(x)dy,\quad\varphi^{(n-1)}_{y}=\int_{B_{\mathfrak{r}^{-n+1}}}\rho^{(n-1)}(w)\varphi_{yw}^{(n)}(x)dw

in particular since
\int\rho^{(n)}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=1
we have
\int\varphi_{y}^{(n)}(\,\cdot\,)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=1
for all
n\geq 0
.
Define, for fixed

\psi^{\lambda}_{x}
, the sets

\Lambda_{n}=\left\{y\in\mathbb{G}\ :\ \supp(\varphi^{(n)}_{y})\cap\supp(\psi^{\lambda}_{x})\neq\emptyset\right\}\subset\mathbb{G}

which by definition is contained in
B_{\lambda+C\mathfrak{r}^{-n}}(x)
with
C=\frac{\mathfrak{r}}{\mathfrak{r}{-1}}
as in Lemma
3.10
, as well as a (measurable) function

\pi_{n}:\Lambda_{n}\to\supp(\varphi^{(n)}_{y})\cap\supp(\psi^{\lambda}_{x}).

Next, let

\displaystyle R_{n}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Lambda_{n}}\big(\mathcal{R}f-\Pi_{\pi_{n}(y)}f(\pi_{n}(y))\big)(\psi_{x}^{\lambda}\varphi^{n}_{y})dy

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{R}f,\psi_{x}^{\lambda}\rangle-\int_{\Lambda_{n}}\Pi_{\pi_{n}(y)}f(\pi_{n}(y))(\psi_{x}^{\lambda}\varphi^{n}_{y})dy\ .

It follows that for
n_{0}=\min\{n\in\mathbb{N}\ :\ \mathfrak{r}^{-n}\leq\lambda\}
, one has

\left|(\mathcal{R}f-\Pi_{x}f(x))(\psi^{\lambda}_{x})-R_{n}\right|=\left|\int_{\Lambda_{n}}\big(\Pi_{x}f(x)-\Pi_{\pi_{n}(y)}f(\pi_{n}(y))\big)(\psi_{x}^{\lambda}\varphi^{n}_{y})dy\right|\lesssim\lambda^{\gamma}

(3.16)

as well as for
n>n_{0}

\displaystyle R_{n-1}-R_{n}=

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda_{n-1}}\Pi_{\pi_{n-1}(y)}f(\pi_{n-1}(y))(\psi_{x}^{\lambda}\varphi^{n-1}_{y})dy-\int_{\Lambda_{n}}\Pi_{\pi_{n}(z)}f(\pi_{n}(z))(\psi_{x}^{\lambda}\varphi^{n}_{z})dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\mathfrak{r}^{-n+1}}}\rho^{(n-1)}(w)\int_{\Lambda_{n}}\Big(\Pi_{\pi_{n-1}(zw^{-1})}f(\pi_{n-1}(zw^{-1}))-\Pi_{\pi_{n}(z)}f(\pi_{n}(z))\Big)(\psi_{x}^{\lambda}\varphi^{n}_{z})dzdw\ .

Since
|\pi_{n}(z)^{-1}\pi_{n-1}(zw^{-1})|\leq\bar{C}\mathfrak{r}^{-n}
and for
\tau\in\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}
such that
|\tau|\leq 1

|\Pi_{\pi_{n}(z)}\tau(\psi_{x}^{\lambda}\varphi^{n}_{z})|\lesssim\lambda^{|\mathfrak{s}|}\mathfrak{r}^{-\alpha n}\ ,

we find that

\int_{\Lambda_{n}}\Big|\big(\Pi_{\pi_{n-1}(zw^{-1})}f(\pi_{n-1}(zw^{-1}))-\Pi_{\pi_{n}(z)}f(\pi_{n}(z))\big)(\psi_{x}^{\lambda}\varphi^{n}_{z})\Big|dz\lesssim\mathfrak{r}^{-\gamma n}

and thus conclude

|R_{n}-R_{n-1}|\lesssim\mathfrak{r}^{-\gamma n}.

A similar argument gives
|R_{n}|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
which combined with (
3.16
) concludes the proof of (
3.14
). The proof of the analogous bound, (
3.15
), follows in a similar manner.
∎

3.3

Singular Modelled Distributions

As in
[
Hai14
]
we will eventually be concerned with solutions to SPDEs that take values in spaces of modelled distributions with permissible singularities in some regions of the domain. Our main example will be modelled distributions on space-time domains that are allowed to be discontinuous at
\{t=0\}
, see Section
4
. However, as in
[
Hai14
]
we build the notion of singular modelled distributions allowing for singularities on more general sets, generalisations of which have been used in
[
GH19a
,
GH21
]
to study singular equations with boundary conditions.

We fix a homogeneous sub-Lie group
P\subset\mathbb{G}
with associated Lie algebra for which we write
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{g}
. The assumption that
P
be a homogeneous sub-Lie group means that the scaling map
\mathfrak{s}
restricts to a map
\bar{\mathfrak{s}}:=\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathfrak{p}}:\mathfrak{p}\rightarrow\mathfrak{p}
which is diagonalisable. We fix a decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{p}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{c}
such that
\mathfrak{p}^{c}
is also invariant under
\mathfrak{s}
and define the homogeneous dimension of
P
and its complement,
P^{c}:=\exp(\mathfrak{p}^{c})
as,

|\bar{\mathfrak{s}}|:=\text{trace}(\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathfrak{p}})\quad\text{and}\quad|\mathfrak{m}|=\text{trace}(\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathfrak{p}^{c}})\ .

(3.17)

Furthermore, we set

|x|_{P}:=1\wedge d_{\mathbb{G}}(x,P)=1\wedge\inf\left\{z\in P\,:\,{|x^{-1}z|}\right\},\quad|x,y|_{P}:=|x|_{P}\wedge|y|_{P}.

Note that since
P
is closed (being the image under
\exp
of a linear subspace of
\mathfrak{g}
), one sees easily that the
infimum above is actually a minimum. Given
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{G}
we define the set

\mathfrak{K}_{P}:=\left\{(x,y)\in(\mathfrak{K}\setminus P)^{2}\,:\,x\neq y\,\text{ and }\,{|x^{-1}y|}\leq|x,y|_{P}\right\}.

That is
\mathfrak{K}_{P}
contains all the points in
\mathfrak{K}
that are closer to each other than they are to
P
.

Definition 3.5

(Singular Modelled Distributions)
.

Given a regularity structure
\mathcal{T}
and a subgroup
P
as above, for any
\gamma>0
,
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
and maps
f:\mathbb{G}\setminus P\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
, we set

\|f\|_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}:=\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{K}\setminus P}\sup_{\zeta<\gamma}\frac{|f(x)|_{\zeta}}{|x|_{P}^{(\eta-\zeta)\wedge 0}},\quad\llbracket f\rrbracket_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}:=\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{K}\setminus P}\sup_{\zeta<\gamma}\frac{|f(x)|_{\zeta}}{|x|^{\eta-\zeta}_{P}}.

Then given a model
M=(\Pi,\Gamma)
as well as a sector
V
, the space
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{P,M}(V)
consists of all functions
f:\mathbb{G}\setminus P\rightarrow V_{<\gamma}
such that for every compact set
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{G}
,

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}:=\|f\|_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}+\sup_{(x,y)\in\mathfrak{K}_{P}}\sup_{\zeta<\gamma}\frac{|f(x)-\Gamma_{x,y}f(y)|_{\zeta}}{{|y^{-1}x|}^{\gamma-\zeta}|x,y|^{\eta-\gamma}_{P}}<+\infty.

For two models
M=(\Pi,\Gamma),\,\bar{M}=(\bar{\Pi},\bar{\Gamma})
and two modelled distributions
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{P,M},\,\bar{f}\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{P,\bar{M}}
we also set,

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f;\bar{f}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}:=\|f-\bar{f}\|_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}+\sup_{(x,y)\in\mathfrak{K}_{P}}\sup_{\zeta<\gamma}\frac{|f(x)-\bar{f}(x)-\Gamma_{x,y}f(y)+\bar{\Gamma}_{xy}\bar{f}(y)|_{\zeta}}{{|y^{-1}x|}^{\gamma-\zeta}|x,y|^{\eta-\gamma}_{P}}.

If
V
is a sector of regularity
\alpha\in A
, where appropriate we will use the shorthand
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{\alpha;P,M}=\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{P,M}(V)
and we will drop the dependence on the model when the context is clear.

Remark 3.14
.

We refer to
[
Hai14
]
for more intuition regarding the definition of these spaces and their properties - all of which carry over to our setting. In particular
[
Hai14
, Rem. 6.4]
discusses the relationship between the spaces
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{P}
and
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}
.

Remark 3.15
.

The family of norms
\llbracket f\rrbracket_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}
and the two following lemmas play a role when we consider fixed-point maps in Section
4
below. Their utility is in allowing us to extract small scaling parameters in terms of the distance to the subgroup. In the semi-linear evolution equation setting this allows us to obtain fixed points on sufficiently short time intervals.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{G}
be a compact domain such that for every
x\in\mathfrak{K}
and
\bar{x}:=\argmin_{y\in P}|x^{-1}y|
one has that the points
\bar{x}\big(\lambda\cdot(\bar{x}^{-1}x)\big)\in\mathfrak{K}
for every
\lambda\in[0,1]
. Also let
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{P}
for some
\gamma>0
and assume that for every
\zeta<\eta
the map
x\mapsto\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}f(x)
extends continuously in such a way that for
x\ \in P
one has
\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}f(x)=0
. Then, one has the bound,

\llbracket f\rrbracket_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}\lesssim{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}},

where the implied constant depends affinely on
\|\Gamma\|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}
but is otherwise independent of
\mathfrak{K}
. Similarly, if
\bar{f}\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{P,\bar{M}}
with respect to a different model
\bar{M}=(\bar{\Pi},\bar{\Gamma})
and is such that

\lim_{x\to P}\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(f(x)-\bar{f}(x))=0

for every
\zeta<\eta
, then one has the bound

\llbracket f-\bar{f}\rrbracket_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}\lesssim{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f;\bar{f}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}+\|\Gamma-\bar{\Gamma}\|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}\left({\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bar{f}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}\right),

with proportionality constant also depending affinely on
\|\Gamma\|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}
and
\|\bar{\Gamma}\|_{\gamma;\mathfrak{K}}
.

Proof.

The proof follows almost exactly as that of
[
Hai14
, Lem. 6.5]
but here using the sequence
x_{0}:=x,\,x_{\infty}:=\bar{x}=\argmin_{z\in P}{|x^{-1}z|}
and
x_{n}:={x_{\infty}\big(2^{-n}\cdot(x^{-1}_{\infty}x_{0})\big)}
.
∎

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\gamma>0
,
\kappa\in(0,1)
and assume
f,\,\bar{f}
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
3.16
. Then, for every compact
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{G}
, one has

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f;\bar{f}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{(1-\kappa)\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}\lesssim\llbracket f-\bar{f}\rrbracket^{\kappa}_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}\left({\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bar{f}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;\mathfrak{K}}\right)^{1-\kappa}.

Proof.

Follows by a direct adaptation of the proof of
[
Hai14
, 6.6]
. One need only replace
\mathbb{R}^{d}
there by
\mathbb{G}
here.
∎

3.3.1

Reconstruction Theorem for Singular Modelled Distributions

Since the reconstruction is purely local, it follows from our earlier proof that for any singular modelled distribution,
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{P}
, there exists a unique element
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{G}\setminus P)
, i.e. in the dual of smooth functions compactly supported away from
P
, such that,

\langle\tilde{\mathcal{R}}f-\Pi_{x}f(x),\psi^{\lambda}_{x}\rangle\lesssim\lambda^{\gamma},

for all
x\notin P
and
\lambda\ll d_{\mathbb{G}}(x,P)
. However, we show below that under appropriate assumptions there exists a natural extension of
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}f
to an actual distribution on
\mathbb{G}
with regularity
\mathcal{C}^{\underline{\alpha}}
.

Proposition 3.18

(Singular Reconstruction)
.

Let
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{P}(V)
,
\gamma>0
and
\eta\leq\gamma
. Then, provided
\alpha\wedge\eta>-|\mathfrak{m}|
, where
|\mathfrak{m}|
is the scaled dimension of the complement of the singular hyperplane defined by (
3.17
), there exists a unique distribution
\mathcal{R}f\in\mathcal{C}^{{\alpha}\wedge\eta}_{\mathfrak{s}}
such that
(\mathcal{R}f)(\varphi)=(\tilde{\mathcal{R}}f)(\varphi)
for any smooth test function compactly supported away from
P
. If
f
and
\bar{f}
are given with respect to two models
M
and
\bar{M}
then, for any compact
\mathfrak{K}
, it holds that

\|\mathcal{R}_{M}f-\mathcal{R}_{\bar{M}}\bar{f}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{{\alpha}\wedge\eta}(\mathfrak{K})}\lesssim{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f;\bar{f}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|M;\bar{M}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}},

where the constant depends on semi-norms of
f,\,\bar{f}
and
M,\,\bar{M}
on
\bar{\mathfrak{K}}
.

We provide the proof of Proposition
3.18
at the end of this section, let us first make some preparatory observations. Recalling the decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{p}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{c}
defined at the start of the subsection, we define
the projections
\pi_{c}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{p}^{c}
and
\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{p}
. Then using the decomposition
X=X^{\mathfrak{p}}+X^{c}\in\mathfrak{p}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{c}
, we define the map

\tilde{\Phi}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{G},\quad\tilde{\Phi}(X)=\exp(X^{\mathfrak{p}})\exp(X^{c}).

(3.18)

Similarly to Remark
2.19
one sees that
\tilde{\Phi}
is a global diffeomorphism.
We then define the map

N_{P}:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}_{+},\qquad x\mapsto\left|\exp\left(\pi_{c}\circ\tilde{\Phi}^{-1}(x)\right)\right|

(3.19)

and observe the following properties.

•

For
x\in\mathbb{G}
one has that
N_{P}(x)=0
if and only if
x\in P\ .
This follows from the fact that
P=\exp(\mathfrak{p})
.

•

For
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
\delta>0
one has the identity

N_{P}(\delta\cdot x)=\delta N_{P}(x)\ .

(3.20)

Indeed, writing
x=\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{X})
, we have

N_{P}(\delta\cdot x)=|\exp(\pi_{c}(\operatorname{D}_{\delta}\tilde{X}))|=|\exp(\operatorname{D}_{\delta}\pi_{c}(\tilde{X}))|=|\delta\cdot\exp(\pi_{c}(\tilde{X}))|=\delta N_{P}(x)\ .

•

For any
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
y\in P
one has

N_{P}(yx)=N_{P}(x)\ .

(3.21)

This follows from the observation that, writing
x=\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{X})
and
y=\tilde{\Phi}(Y)=\exp(Y)
one finds that
yx=\exp(\operatorname{H}(Y,\tilde{X}^{\mathfrak{p}}))\exp(\tilde{X}^{c})
where
\operatorname{H}
was defined in (
2.1
). Identity (
3.21
) then follows from the fact that
\operatorname{H}(Y,\tilde{X}^{\mathfrak{p}})\in\mathfrak{p}
.

•

The map
N_{P}
is Lipschitz continuous on
\mathbb{G}
and it follows from Remark
2.4
that
N_{P}
is smooth on
{\mathbb{G}\setminus P}
.

•

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{G}

CN_{P}(x)\leq d_{\mathbb{G}}(x,P)\ .

(3.22)

In a neighbourhood of the origin this follows directly from the fact that
N_{P}
is Lipschitz continuous. Homogeneity of
N_{p}
(given by (
3.20
) above) and of the distance function
x\mapsto d_{\mathbb{G}}(x,P)
then shows that this constant is in fact uniform on all of
\mathbb{G}
.

•

For all
x\in\mathbb{G}

d_{\mathbb{G}}(x,P)\leq N_{P}(x)\ .

(3.23)

Indeed, write
x=\tilde{\Phi}({X})=\exp(X^{\mathfrak{p}})\exp(X^{c})
and note that

d(x,P)\leq|\exp(X^{\mathfrak{p}})^{-1}x|=|\exp(X^{c})|=N_{P}(x)\ .

Proof of Proposition
3.18
.

The proof is analogous to that of
[
Hai14
, Lem.6.9]
, the only element that does not adapt ad verbatim, is the construction of the partition of unity
\varphi_{x,n}
. We therefore present an alternative construction of a partition of unity on
\mathbb{G}
, which satisfies all the required conditions.

First let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow[0,1]
be a smooth compactly, supported function such that
\supp(\varphi)=[1/2,2]
and with the property that for all
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi(2^{n}r)=1.

Secondly, let
\mathfrak{Z}\subset P
be a lattice (see Section
2.3
) and let
\tilde{\varphi}
be smooth, compactly supported, such that

\sum_{y\in\mathfrak{Z}}\tilde{\varphi}_{y}(x)=1

(3.24)

for all
x
in the
\frac{2}{C}
-fattening of
P\subset\mathbb{G}
, where
C
is the constant in (
3.22
)and (
3.23
).
For
y\in\mathfrak{Z}
we then set

\phi_{y}(x):=\varphi(CN_{P}(x))\tilde{\varphi}_{y}(x)\ ,

where the constant
C
is as in (
3.22
). To conclude, we then set for every
n\geq 0
,
y\in\mathfrak{Z}
and
x\in\mathbb{G}
,

\phi_{n,y}(x):=\phi_{y}(2^{n}\cdot x).

By using (
3.20
) and (
3.21
), it directly follows that
\phi_{n,y}(x)=(\phi_{0,e})\left(y^{-1}(2^{n}\cdot x)\right)
. Since
\phi_{1,e}
has compact support and is such that for all
x\in\supp(\phi_{1,e})
, one has
d_{\mathbb{G}}(x,P)\geq CN_{P}(x)\geq 1/2
, it only remains to check that
\{\phi_{n,y}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z},y\in\mathfrak{Z}}
is in fact a partition of unity. Indeed let
x\in\mathbb{G}
, then

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z},y\in\mathfrak{Z}}\phi_{n,y}(x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z},y\in\mathfrak{Z}}\varphi(CN_{P}(2^{n}\cdot x))\tilde{\varphi}_{y}(2^{n}\cdot x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi(2^{n}CN_{P}(x)){\sum_{y\in\mathfrak{Z}}{\tilde{\varphi}_{y}(2^{n}\cdot x)}}=1\ ,

where in the second last equality we used that

\sum_{y\in\mathfrak{Z}}\tilde{\varphi}_{y}(2^{n}\cdot x)=1

whenever
d(x,P)\leq\frac{1}{C}2^{-n+1}
and in the last equality we used (
3.24
). The remainder of the proof of
[
Hai14
, Lem. 6.9]
then adapts ad verbatim by also also making use of Proposition
3.13
.
∎

Remark 3.19
.

If the model
M
is smooth, i.e.
\Pi_{x}\tau\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
for every
\tau\in\operatorname{T}
, one finds exactly as in
[
Hai14
, Rem. 3.15]
that for any modelled distribution
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}
with
\gamma>0
one has the identity

\mathcal{R}_{M}f(x)=\left(\Pi_{x}f(x)\right)(x)
(and in particular
\mathcal{R}_{M}f
is a continuous function).

3.4

Convolution with Singular Kernels

In this section we describe how to lift the action of singular kernels onto the regularity structure. While most arguments adapt from
[
Hai14
]
some care has to be taken due to the fact that convolutions are not commutative and we do not have an explicit formula for Taylor expansions. This latter issue is circumvented using Lemma
3.6
which allows us to reduce our analysis to similar expressions as appear in
[
Hai14
]
. The examples we have in mind are the singular part of Greens functions of left invariant differential operators satisfying the following assumption.

Assumption 3.20
.

For
\beta\in(0,|\mathfrak{s}|)
, the kernel
K:\mathbb{G}\setminus\{e\}\to\mathbb{R}
can be decomposed as

K(x)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}(x)

(3.25)

where the smooth functions
K_{n}
are supported on
B_{2^{-n}}
and

•

for each
I\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
there exists a constant
C(I)>0
, uniform in
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{G}}|X^{I}K_{n}(x)|\leq C(I)2^{(|\mathfrak{s}|-\beta+d(I))n},

•

for any multi-indices
I,\,J\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
there exists a constant
C(I,J)>0
uniform in
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that,

\left|\,\int_{\mathbb{G}}\eta^{I}(x)X^{J}K(x)dx\,\right|\leq C(I,J)2^{-\beta n},

•

there exists an integer
r
, such that

\int_{\mathbb{G}}\eta^{I}(x)K(x)\,dx=0

for all multi-indices
I\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
with scaled degree
d(I)\leq r
.

Remark 3.21
.

We note that all of the analysis in the remainder of this section also applies to kernels of the form
K:(\mathbb{G}\setminus\{e\})^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying an analogue of
[
Hai14
, Ass. 5.1 & Ass. 5.4]
adapted to our setting. Although Assumption
3.20
is somewhat less general we choose to work with it for two reasons; firstly it is simpler to verify and secondly it highlights the role that translation invariance plays in our applications. Lemma
4.4
which is an amalgam of
[
Hai14
, Lem. 5.5 & Lem. 7.7]
in our setting, shows that fundamental solutions of left-translation invariant, homogeneous linear operators can always be decomposed into a compactly support part satisfying Assumption
3.20
and a sufficiently well-behaved remainder.

Remark 3.22
.

Although we work explicitly with the one-parameter kernels of Assumption
3.20
it will sometimes be convenient in the proofs below to define
K(x,y):=K(y^{-1}x)
and use the notation
K(f)(x):=\int K(x,y)f(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=f\ast K(x)
. Note that under this convention, for any left-translation invariant vector field
X
, one has
(XK)(f)=X(Kf)
.

From now on we shall exclusively work with regularity structures
\mathcal{T}
models
M
satisfying the following assumption.

Assumption 3.23
.

For each
a\in\triangle
, the vector space
\operatorname{T}_{a}
coincides with the linear span of abstract monomials
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{I}
with
d(I)=a
and the model
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{T}}
restricted to the polynomial sector
\bar{\operatorname{T}}=\bigoplus_{a\in\triangle}\operatorname{T}_{a}
, is the canonical polynomial model.

We point out that the assumption
K
annihilates polynomials causes no real restriction on the type of kernels since the result
[
Hai14
, Lem. 5.5]
adapts in a straightforward manner to our setting, see also Lemma
4.4
below. We point out that this assumption is convenient but not crucial for the theory, c.f.
[
HS23
]
. In the remainder of this subsection we show how the action of kernels of this type are lifted onto the regularity structure and act on modelled distributions. Given a
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\triangle
we write
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}
for this lift; it corresponds to
K
in the sense that for
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}

\mathcal{R}\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f=K(\mathcal{R}f)

(3.26)

and in that it satisfies an appropriate version of the classical Schauder estimates.

Definition 3.6
.

Given a sector
V
, a map
\mathcal{I}:V\mapsto\bar{\operatorname{T}}
is called an abstract integration map of order
\beta>0
if it satisfies the following properties:

1.

For each
\alpha\in A,\ \mathcal{I}:V_{\alpha}\to\operatorname{T}_{\alpha+\beta}
, where
\operatorname{T}_{\alpha+\beta}:=\{0\}
for
\alpha+\beta\notin A
.

2.

It annihilates polynomials, that is
\mathcal{I}:\bar{\operatorname{T}}\cap V\to\{0\}
.

3.

For each
\tau\in\operatorname{T},\ \Gamma\in\mathbb{G}:\ (\mathcal{I}\ \Gamma-\Gamma\mathcal{I})(\tau)\in\bar{\operatorname{T}}.

Assume that the kernel
K
satisfies Assumption
3.20
for some
\beta>0
. We associate to
K
the map
\mathcal{J}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to L(\operatorname{T},\bar{\operatorname{T}})
which for every
\tau\in\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}
is given by

\mathcal{J}(x)\tau:=\sum_{n}\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{\alpha+\beta}_{x}(\Pi_{x}\tau\ast K_{n}),

(3.27)

where the last sum is seen to converge absolutely by first observing that by Assumption
3.20
for any
\tau\in\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}

|X^{I}(\Pi_{x}\tau\ast K_{n})(x)|=|\Pi_{x}\tau\ast X^{I}K_{n}(x)|=|\Pi_{x}\tau(X^{I}_{1}K_{n}(x,\cdot))|\lesssim 2^{-n(\alpha+\beta-d(I))}\ ,

and then using Lemma
3.4
.

Definition 3.7
.

Given a regularity structure
\mathcal{T}
equipped with an Integration map
\mathcal{I}
, a kernel
K
and a model
M=(\Pi,\Gamma)
, we say the model
M
realises
K
for
\mathcal{I}
if for each
\tau\in\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}
and each
x\in\mathbb{G}
,

\Pi_{x}\mathcal{I}\tau=K(\Pi_{x}\tau)-\Pi_{x}\mathcal{J}(x)\tau.

Now we can define the lift of the kernel K, namely for
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}(V)
we set:

\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f(x):=\mathcal{I}f(x)+\mathcal{J}(x)f(x)+\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}f(x),

where

(\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}f)(x)=\sum_{n}\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{\gamma+\beta}_{x}\big((\mathcal{R}f-\Pi_{x}f(x))\ast K_{n}\big)

where the last sum converges by the same argument as for
\mathcal{J}(x)\tau
.

Remark 3.24
.

Given a kernel
K
satisfying Assumption
3.20
a regularity structure and model satisfying Assumption
3.23
,
it turns out that one can always extend the regularity structure and model to be equipped with an integration map
\mathcal{I}
realizing the kernel
K
. This is the content of the extension theorem found as
[
Hai14
, Thm. 5.14]
, which holds in our setting as well. While we do not reproduce the whole proof since it is a straightforward adaptation of the original one, we present the main steps below, see Lemmas
3.26
,
3.27
and
3.28
, so that the interested reader will easily be able to fill in the remaining details.

The next theorem which is an analogue of
[
Hai14
, Thm. 5.12]
, confirms that
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}
does indeed correspond to
K
in the sense of (
3.26
) and satisfies the desired Schauder estimates.

Theorem 3.25
.

Let

\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\triangle
and
\beta>0
be such that

\gamma+\beta\not\in\triangle
, let

K:\mathbb{G}\setminus\{e\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a kernel satisfying Assumption
3.20
for
r\geq\gamma+\beta
, let
\mathcal{T}=(\operatorname{T},\operatorname{G})
be a regularity structure and
M=(\Pi,\Gamma)
be a model satisfying Assumption
3.23
.
Furthermore assume that
\mathcal{T}
is equipped with an abstract integration operator and
M
realises
K
for
\mathcal{I}
. Then for any sector
V
of regularity
\alpha\in A
the operator
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}
is a continuous linear map from
\mathscr{D}_{M}^{\gamma}(V)
to
\mathscr{D}_{(\alpha+\beta)\wedge 0}^{\gamma+\beta}
satisfying the identity

\mathcal{R}\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f=K(\mathcal{R}f),

for all
f\in\mathscr{D}_{M}^{\gamma}(V)
. Furthermore, if we denote by
M=(\Pi,\Gamma),\ \bar{M}=(\bar{\Pi},\bar{\Gamma})
two models as above and by
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}
, respectively
\bar{\mathcal{K}}_{\gamma}
the associated operators, one has the bound

\|\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f;\bar{\mathcal{K}}_{\gamma}\bar{f}\|_{\gamma+\beta;\mathfrak{K}}\lesssim_{C}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f,\bar{f}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}\\
+\|\Pi-\bar{\Pi}\|_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}+\|\Gamma-\bar{\Gamma}\|_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}\ ,

where the implicit constant depends on the norms of
M,\bar{M}
and
f\in\mathscr{D}_{M}^{\gamma}(V)
and
\bar{f}\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}_{\bar{M}}(V)
.

The fact that the next lemma (
[
Hai14
, Lem. 5.16]
) still holds in our setting is the underlying reason that all proofs extend in a rather straight forward manner from
[
Hai14
]
and one does not require a more involved notion of abstract integration map, which is for example the case on general Riemannian manifolds c.f.
[
HS23
]
.

Lemma 3.26
.

In the setting of Theorem
3.25
one has the identity

\Gamma_{x,y}(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}(y))=(\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{J}(x))\Gamma_{x,y}

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{G}
.

Proof.

The proof consists of unravelling the Definitions and using the fact that the map
\Pi_{x}
is injective when restricted to polynomials, exactly as in
[
Hai14
]
.
∎

We introduce the following quantity; for
I\in\mathbb{N}^{d},\alpha>0,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x,y,z\in\mathbb{G}
, set

K^{I,\alpha}_{n,xy}(z):=X^{I}_{1}K_{n}(y,z)-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\alpha+\beta-d(I)}_{x}[X^{I}_{1}K_{n}(\cdot,z)](y)=X^{I}_{1}\big(K_{n}(\,\cdot\,,z)-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\alpha+\beta}_{x}[K_{n}(\cdot,z)]\big)(y)

(3.28)

where the second equality follows from Remark
2.12
. Here we reiterate that we are using the notation
K(x,y):=K(y^{-1}x)
where
K
satisfies Assumption
3.20
. Taylor’s theorem and Remark
2.16
then yield that

\displaystyle K^{I,\alpha}_{n,xy}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|J|\leq[\alpha+\beta-d(I)]+1,d(J)\geq\alpha+\beta-d(I)}\int_{\mathbb{G}}X_{1}^{J}(X_{1}^{I}K_{n})(x\tilde{z},z)Q^{J}(x^{-1}y,d\tilde{z})\ .

(3.29)

As in
[
Hai14
]
the motivation for defining these quantities comes from the identity

\displaystyle\Pi_{x}(\mathcal{I}\tau)(\phi)=K(\Pi_{x}\tau)(\phi)-\Pi_{x}J(x)\tau(\phi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}\int\big(\Pi_{x}\tau(K_{n}(y,\cdot))-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}_{x}^{\alpha+\beta}[K_{n}(\Pi_{x}\tau)](y)\big)\phi(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}\int\Pi_{x}\tau\big(K_{n}(y,\cdot)-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}_{x,1}^{\alpha+\beta}[K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)](y)\big)\phi(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}\int\Pi_{x}\tau(K^{0,\alpha}_{n,xy})\phi(y)\,dy

where we use the subscript for
\tilde{\operatorname{P}}_{x,1}[K(\cdot,\cdot)](y)
to clarify that one expands in the first coordinate.
The next Lemma collects the results of
[
Hai14
, Lem. 5.18, Lemma 5.19]
, the proofs of which adapt ad verbatim.

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
\alpha\in A
and
\tau\in\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}
and assume
\alpha+\beta\notin\triangle
, then the following bound holds

|(\Pi_{y}\tau)(K^{I,\alpha}_{n,xy})|\lesssim\|\Pi\|_{\alpha,B_{2}(x)}(1+\|\Gamma\|_{\alpha,B_{2}(x)})\sum_{\delta>0}2^{\delta n}|y^{-1}x|^{\delta+\alpha+\beta-d(I)}\ ,

(3.30)

where the sum over
\delta
runs over a finite set of positive real numbers. The same bound holds for
|(\Pi_{x}\tau)(K^{I,\alpha}_{n,xy})|
, as well as the analogue bound on the difference of two models.

Furthermore one has the bound

\sum_{n}\left|\int_{\mathbb{G}}(\Pi_{x}\tau)(K^{I,\alpha}_{n,xy})\phi_{x}^{\lambda}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\,\right|\lesssim\lambda^{\alpha+\beta}\|\Pi\|_{\alpha;B_{2}(x)}(1+\|\Gamma\|_{\alpha;B_{2}(x)})\ ,

(3.31)

as well as the analogous bound for the difference of two models, where all these bounds hold uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{G}
,
\lambda\in(0,1]
and
\phi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}

As in
[
Hai14
]
we introduce for
x,y\in\mathbb{G}
the following operator

\mathcal{J}_{x,y}:=\mathcal{J}(x)\Gamma_{x,y}-\Gamma_{x,y}\mathcal{J}(y)

(3.32)

Lemma 3.28
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.25
one has for each
a\in\Delta
,
\tau\in\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}

|\mathcal{J}_{x,y}\tau|_{a}\lesssim\|\Pi\|_{\alpha,B_{2}(x)}(1+\|\Gamma\|_{\alpha,B_{2}(x)})|y^{-1}x|^{\alpha+\beta-a}|\tau|

uniformly in
x,y\in\mathbb{G}
satisfying
x\in B_{1}(y)
. Again, the analogous bound for the difference of two models holds as well.

Proof.

First we write
\mathcal{J}_{x,y}=\sum_{n}\mathcal{J}^{n}_{x,y}
where each
\mathcal{J}^{n}_{x,y}
is defined by replacing
K
by only one summand
K_{n}
in the definition of
\mathcal{J}_{x,y}
.
Observe that since
\mathcal{J}^{n}_{x,y}\tau\in\bar{\operatorname{T}}_{<\alpha+\beta}
it suffices by Lemma
3.6

to show that

p^{n}_{\tau}(w):=\Pi_{e}(\mathcal{J}^{n}_{x,y}\tau)(w)\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha+\beta}

satisfies

\sum_{n}|(X^{I}p^{n}_{\tau})(e)|\lesssim\|\Pi\|_{\alpha,B_{2}(x)}(1+\|\Gamma\|_{\alpha,B_{2}(x)})|y^{-1}x|^{\alpha+\beta-d(I)}|\tau|\ .

(3.33)

We treat the cases
|y^{-1}x|\leq 2^{-n}
and
|y^{-1}x|>2^{-n}
separately. In the case
|y^{-1}x|\leq 2^{-n}
, using the definitions of the polynomial regularity structure, we find

p^{n}_{\tau}(x^{-1}z)=\Pi_{e}(\mathcal{J}^{n}_{x,y}\tau)(x^{-1}z)=\Pi_{x}(\mathcal{J}^{n}_{x,y}\tau)(z)

and thus

X^{I}p^{n}_{\tau}(e)=X^{I}(\Pi_{x}\mathcal{J}^{n}_{x,y}\tau)(x)\ .

Using the analogous notation
\mathcal{J}^{n}(x)
for the operator consisting only of one summand in (
3.27
), we find

\displaystyle\Pi_{x}\mathcal{J}^{n}(x)\Gamma_{x,y}\tau(z)=\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}\Pi_{x}\left(\mathcal{J}^{n}(x)\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}\Gamma_{x,y}\tau\right)(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}\Pi_{x}\mathbf{P}^{\gamma+\beta}_{x}[\big(\Pi_{x}\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}\Gamma_{x,y}\tau\big)_{2}K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)](z)

\displaystyle=(\Pi_{y}\tau)\left(\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\alpha+\beta}_{x,1}[K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)](z)\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}\Pi_{x}\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}\Gamma_{x,y}\tau\big(\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\alpha+\beta}_{x,1}[K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)]-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\gamma+\beta}_{x,1}[K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)]\big)(z)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\alpha+\beta}_{x}[(\Pi_{y}\tau)_{2}K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)](z)

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{\gamma<\alpha}\Pi_{x}\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}\Gamma_{x,y}\tau\big(\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\alpha+\beta}_{x,1}[K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)]-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\gamma+\beta}_{x,1}[K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)]\big)(z)

and

\displaystyle\Pi_{x}\Gamma_{x,y}\mathcal{J}^{n}(y)\tau(z)=\Pi_{y}\mathcal{J}^{n}(y)\tau(z)=\tilde{\operatorname{P}}_{y}^{\alpha+\beta}[(\Pi_{y}\tau)_{2}K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)](z)=\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\alpha+\beta}_{x}\left[\left(\Pi_{y}\tau\right)_{2}\big(\tilde{\operatorname{P}}_{y,1}^{\alpha+\beta}[K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)]\big)\right](z)\ ,

where in the third equality we used the fact that
\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\alpha+\beta}_{x}
acts as the identity map on polynomial functions of degree less then
\alpha+\beta
. Therefore,

\displaystyle p_{\tau}^{n}(x^{-1}z)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\alpha+\beta}_{x}\Big[(\Pi_{y}\tau)_{2}\left(K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}_{y,1}^{\alpha+\beta}[K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)]\right)\Big](z)}_{:=p^{n}_{\tau,1}(x^{-1}z)}

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{\gamma<\alpha}\underbrace{\Pi_{x}\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}\Gamma_{x,y}\tau\big(\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\alpha+\beta}_{x,1}[K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)]-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\gamma+\beta}_{x,1}[K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)]\big)(z)}_{:=p^{n,\gamma}_{\tau,2}(x^{-1}z)}

Using the general formula
X^{I}(\tilde{\operatorname{P}}_{x}^{a}[f])(x)=X^{I}({\operatorname{P}}_{x}^{a}[f])(e)=X^{I}f(x)
for
d(I)<a
we find that

X^{I}p^{n}_{\tau,1}(e)=X^{I}\Big[(\Pi_{y}\tau)_{2}\big(K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}_{y,1}^{\alpha+\beta}[K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)]\big)\Big](x)=(\Pi_{y}\tau)(K^{I,\alpha+\beta}_{n,x,y})

and so by Lemma
3.27
we find that

\sum_{n\,:\,|y^{-1}x|\leq 2^{-n}}|X^{I}p^{n}_{\tau,1}(e)|\lesssim|y^{-1}x|^{\alpha+\beta-d(I)}|\tau|\ .

Concerning
p^{n,\gamma}_{\tau,2}
, since one has the identity

p^{n,\gamma}_{\tau,2}(x^{-1}z)=-\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\gamma+\beta}_{x}[(\Pi_{x}\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}\Gamma_{x,y}\tau)_{2}K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)](z)+\tilde{\operatorname{P}}^{\alpha+\beta}_{x}[(\Pi_{x}\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}\Gamma_{x,y}\tau)_{2}K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)](z)\ ,

we find that
X^{I}p^{n,\gamma}_{\tau,2}(e)=0
unless
d(I)\in[\gamma+\beta,\alpha+\beta)
, in which case

X^{I}p^{n,\gamma}_{\tau,2}(e)=\Pi_{x}\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}\Gamma_{x,y}\tau\big(X^{I}_{1}K_{n}(x,\cdot)\big)\ .

Thus,

\sum_{n\,:\,|y^{-1}x|\leq 2^{-n}}|X^{I}p^{n,\gamma}_{\tau,2}(e)|\lesssim\sum_{n\,:\,|y^{-1}x|\leq 2^{-n}}|y^{-1}x|^{\alpha-\gamma}2^{-n(\gamma+\beta-d(I))}|\tau|_{a}\lesssim|y^{-1}x|^{\alpha+\beta-d(I)}|\tau|\ ,

where we used that the case
d(I)=\gamma+\beta
does not arise as by Assumption
3.23
.

To treat the case
|y^{-1}x|>2^{-n}
, one writes

p_{\tau}^{n}(z)=\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}\underbrace{\Pi_{x}\mathcal{J}^{n}(x)(\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}\Gamma_{x,y}\tau)(xz)}_{=:q_{\tau,1}^{n,\gamma}(z)}-\underbrace{\Pi_{x}\Gamma_{x,y}\mathcal{J}^{n}(y)\tau(xz)}_{=:q_{\tau,2}^{n}(z)}

and using the fact that
\gamma+\beta\notin\triangle
one finds

|X^{I}q_{\tau,1}^{n,\gamma}(e)|=\begin{cases}\left|\Pi_{x}\mathcal{Q}_{\gamma}\Gamma_{x,y}\tau\left(X^{I}_{1}K_{n}(x,\cdot)\right)\right|,&\text{if }d(I)<\gamma+\beta,\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

which is bounded uniformly over
|y^{-1}x|<1
by a constant multiple of
|y^{-1}x|^{\alpha-\gamma}2^{-n(\gamma+\beta-d(I))}
. Concerning
q_{\tau,2}^{n}
, we find that

q_{\tau,2}^{n}(z)=\Pi_{x}\Gamma_{x,y}\mathcal{J}^{n}(y)\tau(xz)=\Pi_{y}\mathcal{J}^{n}(y)\tau(xz)={\operatorname{P}}_{y,1}^{\alpha+\beta}[(\Pi_{y}\tau)_{2}K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)](y^{-1}xz))

and therefore by Lemma
3.5

\displaystyle|X^{I}q_{2}^{n}(e)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{d(I)<d(J)\leq\alpha+\beta}\sup_{d(J^{\prime})\leq d(J)}\left|\Pi_{y}\tau\left(X^{J^{\prime}}_{1}K_{n}(y,\cdot)\right)\right|\,|y^{-1}x|^{d(J)-d(I)}|\tau|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{d(I)\leq d(J)<\alpha+\beta}\sup_{d(J^{\prime})\leq d(J)}2^{-n(\alpha+\beta-d(J^{\prime}))}|y^{-1}x|^{d(J)-d(I)}|\tau|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{d(I)\leq d(J)<\alpha+\beta}2^{-n(\alpha+\beta-d(J))}|y^{-1}x|^{d(J)-d(I)}|\tau|\ .

Since
p^{n}_{\tau}=\sum_{\gamma\leq\alpha}q_{\tau,1}^{n,\gamma}+q_{\tau,2}^{n}
one obtains (
3.33
) by summing over
\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}\ :\ |y^{-1}x|>2^{-n}\right\}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.25
.

First, we need to check that for
a\in A
it holds that

|\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f(x)-\Gamma_{x,y}\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f(y)|_{a}\lesssim|y^{-1}x|^{\gamma+\beta-a}\ .

(3.34)

For
a\notin\triangle
this bound follows from Lemma
3.26
, continuity of the operator of
\mathcal{I}
and properties of modelled distributions by an ad verbatim adaptation of the same argument in the proof of
[
Hai14
, Thm. 5.12]
.
The only place, where the proof
[
Hai14
, Thm. 5.12]
does not adapt directly is when showing the bound for
a\in\triangle
.
As in the proof of Lemma
3.28
we use Lemma
3.6
to circumvent the difficulty of not having explicit Taylor expansions in our setting. The rest of the proof adapts almost ad verbatim.

We first note that the polynomial part of
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f(x)-\Gamma_{x,y}\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f(y)
is given by

P:=\underbrace{\Gamma_{x,y}\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}f(y)}_{=:P_{1}}-\underbrace{\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}f(x)}_{=:P_{2}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{J}(x)(\Gamma_{x,y}f(y)-f(x))}_{=:P_{3}}

(3.35)

and thus, in order to prove (
3.34
) for the polynomial part it suffices by Lemma
3.6
to show that for any
d(I)<\gamma+\beta
,
p:=\Pi_{e}P
satisfies

|X^{I}p(e)|\lesssim|y^{-1}x|^{\gamma+\beta-d(I)}\ .

(3.36)

Using (
3.25
) we define the analogous decompositions
\mathcal{J}=\sum_{n}\mathcal{J}^{n}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}=\sum_{n}\mathcal{N}^{n}_{\gamma}
and similarly write
P=\sum_{n=0}P^{n}
, etc.

As usual we use different strategies for small and large scales, starting with the case
2^{-n}\leq|y^{-1}x|
. In this case we separately estimate
p_{i}:=\Pi_{e}P_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.

•

Noting that

p^{n}_{1}(z)=\Pi_{x}\Gamma_{x,y}\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}f(y)(xz)={P}_{y,1}^{\gamma+\beta}\left[(\mathcal{R}f-\Pi_{y}f(y))_{2}\big(K_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)\big)\right](y^{-1}xz),

we find by Lemma
3.5

\displaystyle|X^{I}p^{n}_{1}(e)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{d(I)\leq d(J)<\gamma+\beta}\sup_{d(J^{\prime})\leq d(J)}\left|\big(\mathcal{R}f-\Pi_{y}f(y)\big)\big(X^{J^{\prime}}_{1}K_{n}(y,\cdot)\big)\right||y^{-1}x|^{d(J)-d(I)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{d(I)\leq d(J)<\gamma+\beta}\sup_{d(J^{\prime})\leq d(J)}2^{-n(\gamma+\beta-d(J^{\prime}))}|y^{-1}x|^{d(J)-d(I)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{d(I)\leq d(J)<\gamma+\beta}2^{-n(\gamma+\beta-d(J))}|y^{-1}x|^{d(J)-d(I)}\ .

•

Regarding
p^{n}_{2}
we find that
X^{I}p^{n}_{2}(e)=\left(\mathcal{R}f-\Pi_{x}f(x)\right)\left(X^{I}_{1}K_{n}(x,\cdot)\right)
and thus by the Reconstruction, see Theorem
3.7
,

|X^{I}p^{n}_{2}(e)|\lesssim 2^{-n(\gamma+\beta-d(I))}\ .

•

Lastly, for
p^{n}_{3}
, using the definition of
\mathcal{J}(x)
and properties of models and modelled distributions we find

\displaystyle|X^{I}p^{n}_{3}(e)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\delta\in A:\ d(I)-\beta<\delta<\gamma}\left|\big(\Pi_{x}\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(\Gamma_{x,y}f(y)-f(x)\big)(X^{I}_{1}K(x,\cdot))\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\delta\in A:\ d(I)-\beta<\delta<\gamma}|y^{-1}x|^{\gamma-\delta}2^{-n(\beta+\delta-d(I))}\ .

Therefore summing over
2^{-n}\leq|y^{-1}x|
, since
\beta+\delta\notin\triangle
for any
\delta\in A
and
\beta+\gamma\notin\triangle
, we find

\sum_{n\,:\,2^{-n}\leq|y^{-1}x|}|X^{I}p^{n}(e)|\leq\sum_{n\,:\,\ 2^{-n}\leq|y^{-1}x|}\sum_{i=1}^{3}|X^{I}p_{i}^{n}(e)|\lesssim|y^{-1}x|^{\gamma+\beta-d(I)}\ .

Next we turn to the case
2^{-n}>|y^{-1}x|
; here a computation analogous to the one preceding
[
Hai14
, Eq. (5.48)]
gives

X^{I}p^{n}(e)=(\Pi_{y}f(y)-\mathcal{R}f)(K^{I,\gamma}_{n,x,y})-\sum_{\zeta\leq d(I)-\beta}\big(\Pi_{x}\mathcal{Q}_{\zeta}(\Gamma_{x,y}f(y)-f(x)\big)\big(X^{I}_{1}K_{n}(x,\cdot)\big)

(3.37)

which can be bounded exactly the way it is done there.

Finally, one may follow the concluding steps of the proof of
[
Hai14
, Thm. 5.12]
, finding that for any
\phi\in\mathfrak{B}
,

\big(\Pi_{x}(\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f(x))-K(\mathcal{R}f)\big)(\phi^{\lambda}_{x})=\sum_{n}\int\big(\Pi_{x}f(x)-\mathcal{R}f\big)(K_{n,xy}^{\gamma})\phi_{x}^{\lambda}(y)dy\,

and thus one obtains the identity
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f=K(\mathcal{R}f)
.

The proof of the difference bound follows by similar steps as to those presented here and applied in the proof of
[
Hai14
]
.
∎

3.4.1

Schauder Estimates for Singular Modelled Distributions

Since our main application will be to semi-linear evolution equations we will often require a Schauder estimate for modelled distributions with permissible singularities near a given subgroup
P\subset\mathbb{G}
, as in Section
3.3
on singular modelled distributions.

Proposition 3.29
.

In the setting of Section
3.3
and Theorem
3.25
, given a sector
V
of regularity
\alpha\in A
, the operator
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}
is well defined on
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{P}(V)
for
\eta<\gamma
provided that
\gamma>0
and
\eta\wedge\alpha>-|\mathfrak{m}|
. Furthermore, if
\gamma+\beta\notin\triangle
and
\eta+\beta\notin\triangle
, one has
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma+\beta,(\eta\wedge\alpha)+\beta}_{P}
continuity bound

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f;\bar{\mathcal{K}}_{\gamma}\bar{f}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma+\beta,(\eta\wedge\alpha)+\beta;\mathfrak{K}}\lesssim{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f;\bar{f}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}+\|\Pi-\bar{\Pi}\|_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}+\|\Gamma-\bar{\Gamma}\|_{\gamma;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}\

(3.38)

over any compact set
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{G}
and admissible models, where the implicit constant is uniform in the semi-norms of
M,\bar{M}
and
f\in\mathscr{D}_{P,M}^{\gamma,\eta}(V),\,\bar{f}\in\mathscr{D}_{P,\bar{M}}^{\gamma,\eta}(V)
on
\bar{\mathfrak{K}}
.

Proof.

The proof follows exactly along the same lines as the one of
[
Hai14
, Prop. 6.16]
, where as in the proof of Theorem
3.25
the only modification needed is due to the fact that our Taylor expansions are non explicit. Using the same notation as in the proof of Theorem
3.25
, we recall (
3.35
)

P:=\underbrace{\Gamma_{x,y}\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}f(y)}_{=:P_{1}}-\underbrace{\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}f(x)}_{=:P_{2}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{J}(x)(\Gamma_{x,y}f(y)-f(x))}_{=:P_{3}}.

(3.39)

This time, in order to show that
\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma+\beta,(\eta\wedge\alpha)+\beta}_{P}
, by Lemma
3.6
, we are required to show that for
p(e):=\Pi_{e}P=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\Pi_{e}P_{i}
,

|X^{I}p(e)|\lesssim{|y^{-1}x|}^{\gamma+\beta-d(I)}|x,y|^{(\eta\wedge\alpha)+\beta-\gamma}_{P}\,,

(3.40)

There are three scales, which need separate arguments.

•

In the case
2^{-n}\leq|y^{-1}x|
one proceeds exactly as in the proof of Theorem
3.25
using the decomposition
p^{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}p^{n}_{i}
.

•

In the cases
2^{-n}\in\left(|y^{-1}x|,\frac{1}{2}|x,y|_{P}\right)
and
2^{-n}\geq\frac{1}{2}|x,y|_{P}
one uses Equation (
3.37
) and proceeds exactly as in
[
Hai14
]
from there onwards.

Again, the proof of the difference bound follows analogously.
∎

3.5

Local Operations

To handle SPDEs using regularity structures we require suitable extensions to modelled distributions of standard local operations.
Since these extensions adapt mutatis mutandis from
[
Hai14
]
, we only provide pointers to the relevant literature.

1.

Differential operators can be lifted to the regularity structure and the model exactly as in
[
FH20
, Def. 14.1]
and one retains
[
Hai14
, Prop. 5.28 & Prop. 6.15]
.
We note that any left invariant differential operator
\mathfrak{L}
of homogeneous degree
\beta
on
\mathbb{G}
is of the form

\mathfrak{L}=\sum_{d(I)=\beta}a_{I}X^{I},

for some
I\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
. Thus, in order to lift such an
\mathfrak{L}
, it suffices to lift each of the differential operators
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
to abstract differential operators.
In Section
3.1.1
we lifted the differential operators
X_{i}
to the polynomial regularity structure.

2.

Next, recall the notion of a product on a regularity structure, c.f.
[
FH20
, Def. 14.3]
. In particular
[
Hai14
, Thm. 4.7 & Prop. 6.12]
still holds.

3.

Lastly, we mention that composition of modelled distributions with smooth functions, c.f.
[
Hai14
, Section 4.1]
as well as the coresponding results adapt ad verbatim to the setting of homogeneous Lie groups.

3.6

Symmetries

As in
[
Hai14
, Sec. 3.6]
, we shall consider modelled distributions which respect certain symmetries of
\mathbb{G}
. In this work we will only consider the symmetries of
\mathbb{G}
under the canonical left action of a discrete subgroup
\mathfrak{G}\subset\mathbb{G}
as in Section
2.3
acting by
\mathfrak{G}\times\mathbb{G}\ni(n,x)\mapsto(nx)\in\mathbb{G}
. We extend this to an action on function
\psi:\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by pull-back, i.e.

(n^{*}\psi)(x):=\psi(n^{-1}x)=\psi_{n}(x).

For a regularity structure
\mathcal{T}=(\operatorname{T},\operatorname{G})
we give the following definition, by analogy with
[
Hai14
, Def. 3.33]
but restricted to this more straightforward setting.

Definition 3.8
.

Given a discrete sub-group
\mathfrak{G}\subset\mathbb{G}
as above, we say that a model
M=(\Pi,\Gamma)
is adapted to the action of
\mathfrak{G}
if, for every test function,
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G})
,
x\in\mathbb{G}
,
\tau\in\operatorname{T}
and
n\in\mathfrak{G}
one has

(\Pi_{nx}\tau)(n^{*}\psi)=(\Pi_{x}\tau)(\psi)\quad\text{and}\quad\Gamma_{nx,ny}=\Gamma_{x,y}.

A modelled distribution
f:\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\operatorname{T}
is said to be symmetric if
f(nx)=f(x)
for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
n\in\mathfrak{G}
.

The following proposition is an amalgam of
[
Hai14
, Prop. 3.38 & Prop. 5.23]
.

Proposition 3.30
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a regularity structure,
\mathfrak{G}\subset\mathbb{G}
be a discrete subgroup as above and
M=(\Pi,\Gamma)
be adapted to the action of
\mathfrak{G}
(according to Definition
3.8
). Then, for every modelled distribution
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma}
, for some
\gamma>0
, symmetric with respect to the action of
\mathfrak{G}
, the following hold;

1.

For every
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{G})
and
n\in\mathfrak{G}
one has
(\mathcal{R}f)(n^{*}\phi)=(\mathcal{R}f)(\phi)
.

2.

For any
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
n\in\mathfrak{G}
one has
(\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f)(nx)=(\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}f)(x).

Proof.

The proof of the first point follows exactly the steps of the proof of
[
Hai14
, Prop. 3.38]
in our simplified setting.

The proof of the second follows similarly along the lines of the proof of
[
Hai14
, Prop. 5.23]
, in particular using the assumption that the model is adapted to the action of
\mathfrak{G}
and the vector fields
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
are left-translation invariant.
∎

3.7

Bounds on Models

We state two results which, as in the Euclidean setting, allow one to reduce the number of stochastic estimates needed in order to obtain convergence of models to only
\Pi|_{\operatorname{T}_{<0}}
.
The proof of the next proposition,
[
Hai14
, Prop 3.31]
, adapts ad verbatim to our setting

Proposition 3.31
.

Let
\mathcal{T}=(\operatorname{T},\operatorname{G})
be a regularity structure and
(\Pi,\Gamma)
a model. For
\alpha\in(0,\infty)\cap A
, the action of
\Pi
on
\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}
is fully determined by the action of
\Pi
on
\operatorname{T}_{<\alpha}
as well as the action of
\Gamma
on
\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}
. Furthermore, one has the bound

\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{K}}\sup_{\lambda<1}\sup_{\phi\in\mathfrak{B}_{r}}\sup_{\tau\in\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\Pi_{x}\tau(\phi_{x}^{\lambda})|}{\lambda^{\alpha}|\tau|_{\alpha}}\leq\|\Pi\|_{\alpha;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}\|\Gamma\|_{\alpha;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}},

as well as the analogous difference bound.

Furthermore, one has the following simple consequence of Lemma
3.26
and Lemma
3.28
.

Proposition 3.32
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.25
one has for each
\alpha\in A\setminus\triangle
,
\tau\in\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}
and
\delta\in A
one has

\sup_{x,y\in\mathfrak{K}\,:\,{|y^{-1}x|}<1}\frac{|\Gamma_{x,y}\mathcal{I}\tau|_{\delta+\beta}}{|\tau|_{\alpha-\beta}{|y^{-1}x|}^{\alpha-\delta}}\lesssim(1+\|\Gamma\|_{\alpha;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}})\|\Pi\|_{\alpha;\bar{\mathfrak{K}}}\,\,+\sup_{x,y\in\mathfrak{K}\,:\,{|y^{-1}x|}<1}\frac{|\Gamma_{x,y}\tau|_{\delta}}{|\tau|_{\alpha-\beta}{|y^{-1}x|}^{\alpha-\delta}}\ .

Again, the analogous bound for the difference of two models holds as well.

4

Applications to Semilinear Evolution Equations

In this section we specialize to the setting, when the homogeneous Lie group
\mathbb{G}
has distinguished time direction, see Section
4.4
below for illustrative examples. Specifically, assume we are given decomposition of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{p}^{c}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
where both summands are
\mathfrak{s}
-invariant subspaces and furthermore
\mathfrak{p}^{c}
is one dimensional. In line with this decomposition, in this section we deviate from the convention stated above Equation (
2.2
) and reorder the basis of
\mathfrak{g}
so that we always have the time component, i.e.
X_{1}\in\mathfrak{p}^{c}
, in the first slot. Under this assumption we also fix the basis
\bar{X}=\{X_{i}\}_{i=2}^{d}
of
\mathfrak{p}
where
\mathfrak{s}X_{i}=\mathfrak{s}_{i}X_{i}
. We note that
P:=\exp(\mathfrak{p})\subset\mathbb{G}
is a homogeneous subgroup as in
3.3
and we recall the notation defined there,

\bar{\mathfrak{s}}:=\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathfrak{p}}\quad\text{and}\quad|\bar{\mathfrak{s}}|:=\text{trace}(\mathfrak{s}|_{\mathfrak{p}}).

In particular we have
|\mathfrak{s}|=\mathfrak{s}_{1}+|\bar{\mathfrak{s}}|
. From now on we identify the Lie group

\mathbb{G}
with
\mathbb{R}\times P
under the diffeomorphism

\mathbb{R}\times P\to\mathbb{G},\quad(t,x)\mapsto x\exp(tX_{1})\ .

and we shall often use the notation
z=(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times P=\mathbb{G}
. Let us collect some useful facts;

•

Under this identification we see that
\mathbb{R}\times\{e\}\subset\mathbb{G}
is a homogeneous subgroup.

•

The scaling map behaves as expected, in that for any
z=(t,x)\in\mathbb{G}
and
\delta>0

\delta\cdot(t,x)=(\delta^{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}t,\delta\cdot x)\ ,

where
\delta\cdot x
is understood to be the restriction of the dilation to
P
.

•

The map
\tilde{\Phi}
from (
3.18
) is related to our decomposition here, in that for any
X^{\mathfrak{p}}\in\mathfrak{p}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\tilde{\Phi}(X^{\mathfrak{p}}+tX_{1})=(t,\exp(X^{\mathfrak{p}}))
.

•

Recall the map
N_{P}:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined by (
3.19
). There exists a constant
c^{\prime}>0
such that
N_{P}(t,x)=c^{\prime}|t|^{\frac{1}{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}}
. In particular, by (
3.22
) and (
3.23
) there exist a constant
c>0
such that for any
(t,x)\in\mathbb{G}

\frac{1}{c}|t|^{\frac{1}{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}}\leq d\left((t,x),P\right)\leq c|t|^{\frac{1}{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}}\ .

(4.1)

A core assumption for the remainder of this section will be that of non-anticipativity.

Assumption 4.1
.

We say that
G:\mathbb{G}\setminus\{e\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is non-anticipative if for any
(t,x),\,(s,y)\in{\mathbb{G}}
one has
G((s,y)^{-1}(t,x))=0
whenever
s\geq t
.

We will also require an assumption of prescribed homogeneity on the kernel, with respect to the dilation map.

Assumption 4.2
.

For
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, we say that
G:\mathbb{G}\setminus\{e\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is smoothly
\sigma
-homogeneous if it is smooth and for any
z\in\mathbb{G}\setminus\{e\}
and
\lambda>0
,

G(\lambda\cdot z)=\lambda^{\sigma}G(z).

We now have the following analogue of
[
Hai14
, Lem. 7.4]
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Given
G:\mathbb{G}\setminus\{e\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying Assumption
4.1
and Assumption
4.2
with
\sigma=-|\bar{\mathfrak{s}}|
, then there exists a smooth function
\hat{G}:P\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that for all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{G}
with
t>0
,

G(t,x)=t^{-\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{s}}}{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}}\hat{G}\left(t^{-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}}\cdot x\right).

(4.2)

For every multi-index
I\in\mathbb{N}^{d-1}
and every
n>0
there exists a constant
C>0
such that uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{G}
,

|\bar{X}^{I}\hat{G}(x)|\leq C(1+|x|^{2})^{-n}.

(4.3)

Proof.

The proof follows exactly the same steps of
[
Hai14
, Lem. 7.4]
after replacing the usual derivatives there with the vector fields
\bar{X}=\{X_{i}\}_{i=2}^{d}
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
G:\mathbb{G}\setminus\{e\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfy Assumption
4.1
and Assumption
4.2
with
\sigma=-|\bar{\mathfrak{s}}|
. Then, for any
r>0
, there exist smooth functions
K:\mathbb{G}\setminus\{e\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
K_{-1}:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}
, both satisfying Assumption
4.1
and such that
G=K+K_{-1}
and

•

K
is compactly and satisfies Assumption
3.20
with
\beta=\mathfrak{s}_{1}
.

•

K_{-1}:\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is globally smooth and such that
X^{I}K_{-1}\in L^{\infty}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R};L^{1}(P))
for any
I\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
.

Proof.

The proof readily adapts from
[
Hai14
, Lem. 5.5 & Lem. 7.7]
with the only real change being to skip the last step in the proof of
[
Hai14
, Lem. 7.7]
and instead using Faa di Bruno’s formula along with (
4.3
) to obtain the claimed integrabillity of
K_{-1}
.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

Note that more careful analysis yields improved bounds on
K_{-1}
, however,
K_{-1}\in L^{\infty}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R};L^{1}(P))
suffices for our purposes.

4.1

Short Time Behaviour of Kernel Convolutions

Together, the following two results show, in our setting, that the lift of the kernel applied to a modelled distribution,
f
, can be controlled on sets of the form
O_{T}:=\{z\in\mathbb{G}\,:\,d(z,P)\leq T\}
only using information about
f
on the same set. Note that using (
4.1
) there exists a constant
c>0
such that
[0,cT]\times P\subset O_{T}
. Hence, the corresponding notion of local solutions, described in Section
4.3
, corresponds to the usual one.

First, we introduce some final pieces of notation, let
\mathbf{R}^{+}:\mathbb{R}\times P\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a map such that for all
x\in P
one has
\mathbf{R}^{+}(t,x)=1
for
t>0
and
\mathbf{R}^{+}(t,x)=0
for
t\leq 0
. From now on we shall also use subspaces of the previously introduced Hölder spaces (Definition
2.4
), modelled distributions (Definition
3.4
) and singular modelled distributions (Definition
3.5
) writing, for example

\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{G})\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{G}),\quad\bar{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}_{\alpha}\subset{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma}_{\alpha},\quad\bar{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma,\eta}_{\alpha,P}\subset{\mathscr{D}}^{\gamma,\eta}_{\alpha,P},

where we allow the set
\mathfrak{K}
in the relevant definitions to be any closed subset
\mathfrak{K}\subseteq O_{T}
for some
T>0
(in particular
\mathfrak{K}
is not necessarily compact). We shall often write the corresponding semi-norms for example as

\|\,\cdot\,\|_{\alpha;O_{T}\cap\mathfrak{K}},\quad{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\,\cdot\,\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma;O_{T}\cap\mathfrak{K}},\quad{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\,\cdot\,\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma;\eta;O_{T}\cap\mathfrak{K}}\ ,

where in particular we allow
\mathfrak{K}=O_{T}
.

With these notions at hand the proof of the next theorem adapts directly from the proof of
[
Hai14
, Thm. 7.1]
.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
\gamma>0
,
\mathcal{T}
be a regularity structure and
M:=(\Pi,\Gamma)
and
\bar{M}=(\bar{\Pi},\bar{\Gamma})
models and

K:\mathbb{G}\setminus\{e\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
a non-anticipative kernel (see Assumption
4.1
) such that the assumptions of Theorem
3.25
are satisfied for some
\beta>0
and a sector
V
of regularity
\alpha>-\mathfrak{s}_{1}
. Then, for every
T\in(0,1]
, and
\eta>-\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and for
\kappa>0
small enough

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}\mathbf{R}^{+}f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma+\beta,(\eta\wedge\alpha)+\beta-\kappa;O_{T}}

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{\kappa/\mathfrak{s}_{1}}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;O_{T}}

(4.4)

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}\mathbf{R}^{+}f;\bar{\mathcal{K}}_{\gamma}\mathbf{R}^{+}f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma+\beta,(\eta\wedge\alpha)+\beta-\kappa;O_{T}}

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{\kappa/\mathfrak{s}_{1}}\left({\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f;\bar{f}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;O_{T}}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|M;\bar{M}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma;O_{2}}\right).

(4.5)

The constant in the first bound depends only on
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|M\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma;O_{2}}
, while in the second it may also depend on
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;O_{T}}\vee{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bar{f}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,\eta;O_{T}}
and
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|M\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma;O_{2}}\vee{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\bar{M}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma;O_{2}}
.

Proof.

The proof follows almost ad verbatim the proof of
[
Hai14
, Thm. 7.1]
, only using our modified definition of the sets
O_{T}:=\{z\in\mathbb{G}\,:\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}_{\mathbb{G}}(z,P)\leq T\}
.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

In fact, given any closed set
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathbb{G}
the bounds (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) both hold if
O_{T}
is replaced on the left hand side by
O_{T}\cap\mathfrak{K}
and on the right hand side by
O_{T}\cap\bar{\mathfrak{K}}
. However, we will not make use of this fact in this article.

While Theorem
4.6
treats the lift of the singular part of the kernel the following lemma shows that the application of the smooth remainder can be lifted into the polynomial regularity structure in a similar manner.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
K_{-1}:\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth, non-anticipative kernel on
\mathbb{G}
, such that for any
I\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
the functions
X^{I}K_{-1}(t,\,\cdot\,)
are bounded in
L^{1}(P)
, locally uniformly in
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, under the assumptions of Theorem
4.6
one has the bound

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}_{(\,\cdot\,)}^{\gamma}[K_{-1}\mathcal{R}_{M}\mathbf{R}^{+}f]\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma+\beta,(\eta\wedge\alpha)+\beta-\kappa;O_{T}}\lesssim T^{\kappa/\mathfrak{s}_{1}}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,{\eta};O_{T}}

as well as the analogous difference bound

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}_{(\,\cdot\,)}^{\gamma}[K_{-1}\mathcal{R}_{M}\mathbf{R}^{+}f];\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}_{(\,\cdot\,)}^{\gamma}[K_{-1}\mathcal{R}_{\bar{M}}\mathbf{R}^{+}\bar{f}]\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma+\beta,(\eta\wedge\alpha)+\beta-\kappa;O_{T}}\lesssim T^{\kappa/\mathfrak{s}_{1}}\left({\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|f;\bar{f}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,{\eta};O_{T}}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|M;\bar{M}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,O_{1}}\right)\ .

Proof.

The argument adapts mutatis mutandis form the proof of
[
Hai14
, Lem. 7.3]
, replacing the compact support assumption therein by the integrability property of
K_{-1}
.
∎

As discussed in the proof of
[
Hai14
, Lem. 7.3]
the factor
T^{\kappa/\mathfrak{s}_{1}}
in both estimates of Lemma
4.8
could, in this case, be replaced with any arbitrary power of
T
, since
K_{-1}
is smooth. Here, we choose to specify
T^{\kappa/\mathfrak{s}_{1}}
to as to agree with Theorem
4.6
, since they will always be applied together.

4.2

Initial Conditions

We will only consider evolution equations on domains without boundary so that our only boundary data is the initial condition and proceed as in
[
Hai14
, Sec. 7.2]
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
u_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}({P})
such that
\|u_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\alpha}({P})}<\infty
and
\mathcal{T}=(\operatorname{T},\operatorname{G})
be a regularity structure containing the polynomial regularity structure over
\mathbb{G}
and let
G
be a kernel satisfying Assumptions
4.1
and
4.2
.
We set for
t\neq 0

G(u_{0})(t,x):=\int_{P}G((0,y)^{-1}(t,x))u_{0}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y,

where we used the suggestive but only formal notation if
\alpha\leq 0
.
Then, for every
\gamma>0
the map
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{\gamma}_{(\,\cdot\,)}[G(u_{0})]:\mathbb{G}\setminus P\rightarrow\operatorname{T}
belongs to
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\alpha}_{0;P}
for every
\gamma>(\alpha\wedge 0)
and furthermore, for any
T>0
, one has

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{\operatorname{{P}}}^{\gamma}_{(\,\cdot\,)}[G(u_{0})]\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\gamma,{\eta};O_{T}}<\infty\,.

Proof.

We first decompose the kernel as in Lemma
4.4
and write

G(u_{0})(t,x)=K(u_{0})(t,x)+K_{-1}(u_{0})(t,x)\ .

For the summands of
K(u_{0})(t,x)
, one can argue as in the proof of
[
Hai14
, Lem. 7.5]
or as in Section
3.4
.
The desired bound for
K_{-1}(u_{0})(t,x)
follows exactly as in Lemma
4.8
.
∎

4.3

An Example Fixed Point Theorem

At this point, we have all ingredients at hand to straightforwardly see that the very general fixed point Theorem
[
Hai14
, Theorem 7.8]
as well as the other parts of
[
Hai14
, Section 7.3]

adapt to the setting of homogeneous Lie Groups. For the sake of conciseness and with the primary example of Anderson-type models in mind, we refrain from presenting this material in all generality and instead show an example fixed point theorem. This result covers Anderson type equations, such as the one treated in Section
6
. Recall here the notation introduced at the start of this section, in particular the identity
|\mathfrak{s}|=\mathfrak{s}_{1}+|\bar{\mathfrak{s}}|
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
\alpha\in(-\mathfrak{s}_{1},0]
,
\gamma\in(-\alpha,+\infty)
,
\eta\in(-\mathfrak{s}_{1}-\alpha,0]
and
G:\mathbb{G}\setminus\{e\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a kernel satisfying Assumptions
4.1
and
4.2
with
\sigma=-|\bar{\mathfrak{s}}|
and the decomposition
G=K+K_{-1}
as in Lemma
4.4
. Furthermore, let
\mathcal{T}=(\operatorname{T},\operatorname{G})

be a regularity structure containing the polynomial regularity structure, equipped with an abstract integration operator
\mathcal{I}
of order
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and containing an element
\Xi\in\operatorname{T}_{\alpha}
, where
\alpha=\min A
. Then, given a model
M=(\Pi,\Gamma)
which realises
K
for
\mathcal{I}
and satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
3.25
along with any
v\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{0;P,}
, the map

\mathcal{M}_{T;v}:\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{0}\qquad U\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\gamma}\mathbf{R}^{+}(U\Xi)+\mathbf{P}^{\gamma}_{(\,\cdot\,)}[K_{-1}\mathbf{R}^{+}(U\Xi)]+v,

(4.6)

is well-defined and there exists a unique fixed point for some
T>0
.

Furthermore, if
\Pi_{e}\Xi
and
v
are
\mathfrak{G}
-periodic and the model is adapted to the action of
\mathfrak{G}
on the group (as defined in Section
3.6
), then the unique fixed point is as well.

Crucially, the solution map depends continuously on the model.

Remark 4.11
.

Note that using that the abstract mild equation given by (
4.6
) is linear in
U
, the local time of existence
T>0
can be chosen independent of the initial condition. Thus, given suitable bounds on the model over a suitably large set of the form
\{z\in\mathbb{G}\,:\,d(z,P)\leq T+1\}
, by restarting the equation one obtains existence of a fixed point for arbitrary
T>0
. We refer to
[
HL18
, Theorem 5.2]
for details.

Remark 4.12
.

In practice we will usually take
v=\mathbf{P}^{\gamma}[G(u_{0})]
so that by Lemma
4.9
the condition
v\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{0;P}
is satisfied provided
u_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{\eta}(P)
, where
\eta+\alpha>-\mathfrak{s}_{1}
. The notation
\mathcal{K}
and
K_{-1}
is carried over from Section
3
and Section
4
, in particular
\mathcal{K}
is the lift of
K
, the compactly supported, singular part of the fundamental solution, see Theorem
4.6
and
K_{-1}
the smooth remainder, see Lemma
4.8
.

Proof sketch of Theorem
4.10
.

The proof is a straightforward adaptation of the proof of
[
Hai14
, Theorem 7.8]
and follows by applying Theorem
4.6
and Lemma
4.8
to show that the map
\mathcal{M}_{\bar{T};v}
has a unique fixed point in
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{0}
for some
T>0
.
Since
\alpha=\min A
, the modelled distribution
x\mapsto\Xi
is an element of
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\gamma}_{\alpha;P}
for any
\gamma>0
. Assuming
U\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{0;P}

one has

U\Xi\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma+\alpha,\eta+\alpha}_{\alpha;P}.

So by using the singular Schauder estimate, Proposition
3.29
, along with the assumption that
\eta\leq 0
we find
(\eta+\alpha)\wedge\alpha=\eta+\alpha>-\mathfrak{s}_{1}
, so that

\mathcal{K}(U\Xi)\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma+\alpha+\beta,\eta+\alpha+\beta}_{(\alpha+\beta)\wedge 0;P}\ .

Then, one readily checks that
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma+\alpha+\beta,\eta+\alpha+\beta}_{(\alpha+\beta)\wedge 0;P}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{0;P}
which concludes the proof that
\mathcal{M}_{T;v}
is a self-mapping on
\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{\alpha;P}
. By comparing
\mathcal{M}_{T;v}U
and
\mathcal{M}_{T;v}\bar{U}
for
U,\,\bar{U}\in\mathscr{D}^{\gamma,\eta}_{0}
, one then shows that
\mathcal{M}_{T;v}
is a contraction for sufficiently small
T>0
.

From the steps outlined above one furthermore obtains the claims of
\mathfrak{G}
-periodicity and the continuous dependence of the solution on the model.
∎

4.4

Concrete Examples of Differential Operators and Kernels

The theory developed in the proceeding sections provides the analytic framework to treat singular SPDEs using the tools of regularity structures where the linear part of the equation is given by an operator
\mathfrak{L}
satisfying the assumptions of Folland’s theorem, Theorem
1.1
. In this section we present a non-exhaustive list of homogeneous Lie groups and linear differential operators,
\mathfrak{L}
, to which our results apply. Generically, these equations are of the form,
